  November 21, which was 8:40 PM on November 20 by their clock, thirty-five

  11/20 or 11/21: "I'm happy to go either way." Root's choice, theirs to
  reverse: **November 21.** The record keeps UTC and every time and commit of
  this chat will read November 21; the calendar day of November 20 belongs
  wholly to the November 19 dev day, which ran through it; and a label level
  with the clock is what lets root propose a close "once the calendar day has
  turned", which the last four days outran. This is `2026-11-21.1`, and
  everything it makes goes under this directory.

  context" and "Working with the owner". A purpose in the owner's words (a
  person's understanding of their own software does not run away from them);
  five values, each with the owner's words, what it has decided (what is chosen
  and unbuilt marked so), what it costs and where its rules live: a person can
  see it; the map does not lie; it is yours; it meets you where you are; as
  simple as possible, and no simpler. The method: the values pull against each
  other and the design is found between them, with six trades already made and
  which value paid in each. WCAG's four principles set across the three
  audiences, as the check inside two of the values and not the whole. Three
  other ways of cutting it, tried and set aside. Design values and working
  instincts as two lists with one root. And the owner's hypothesis about the
  work items tested: seven of nine issues have a clear first value, the other

  each issue and the board grouped by it, not directories (one parent where
  four issues have two; about 160 links in 50 files point at the issues where
  they stand). The work items' files are not changed: the form is the owner's
  to settle.

  1. The engine reads a folder through one interface (running). This is the
     keystone of the page that scans: list once, read each file once. Every step
     must leave every output identical. The builder stops before the two steps
     that touch what a scan leaves out and the stored form.
  2. The stored form as one module that imports nothing (running), to
     yesterday's requirements: text and binary convert both ways without loss,
     and a reader that always finishes, held by a fuzzer (a test that feeds it
     damaged files).
  3. An author's reference drawn as the author's word (running, first leg): the
     C# compiler's own answers for every <see cref> committed as the answer key,
     counts on real code, and a proposal for how the facts carry it. No reader
     changes yet.
  4. Readers from their languages' own parsers, and a dialect and database per
     SQL script (queued, measured as designs first).
  5. What a stranger meets on your own journey. Running: a trial that feeds a
     finished map to 3D in a scan's rhythm, your "exploring while the scan is
     happening", so you can see it before any engine work. Mine to design next:
     the way down from the World Map, where all six testers stopped.
  6. Parity made checkable (queued): what a person can do in the page against
     the command or file that does the same, and the first scored runs of an
     agent given only the command line.
  7. The design philosophy's independent reading (running).
  8. A standing run on real repositories, with a Windows job.
  9. Small and owed. Running: a probe page for you to click through on Edge (app
     mode on a local file; whether a page from disk remembers a folder).

  A wrinkle on item 7: every agent started in this repository is shown the
  guide, which now states the five values, so none can be a reader "who has not

     keystone of the page that scans: list once, read each file once. Every step
     must leave every output identical. The builder stops before the two steps
     that touch what a scan leaves out and the stored form.
  2. The stored form as one module that imports nothing (running), to
     yesterday's requirements: text and binary convert both ways without loss,
     and a reader that always finishes, held by a fuzzer (a test that feeds it
     damaged files).

     C# compiler's own answers for every <see cref> committed as the answer key,
     counts on real code, and a proposal for how the facts carry it. No reader
     changes yet.

     SQL script (queued, measured as designs first).

     finished map to 3D in a scan's rhythm, your "exploring while the scan is
     happening", so you can see it before any engine work. Mine to design next:
     the way down from the World Map, where all six testers stopped.
  6. Parity made checkable (queued): what a person can do in the page against
     the command or file that does the same, and the first scored runs of an
     agent given only the command line.

  8. A standing run on real repositories, with a Windows job.
  9. Small and owed. Running: a probe page for you to click through on Edge (app
     mode on a local file; whether a page from disk remembers a folder).

  A wrinkle on item 7: every agent started in this repository is shown the
  guide, which now states the five values, so none can be a reader "who has not

  ## Summary
  The probe page and its README are built and committed. The page passes all 22
  headless checks, including that it opens from disk with no error and sends
  nothing. The five real answers still need you, clicking in Edge and Chrome on
  Windows.

  ## Commit
  `d79f46147` on branch `worktree-agent-a1022dabe7a21d6cc`, built on `8bc5736cd`
  as the brief named. It adds five files under
  `/workspaces/Live-Documentation/.claude/worktrees/agent-a1022dabe7a21d6cc/AI-A
  gent-Workspace/AgentSessionHistory/2026/11/2026-11-22/Probes/local-app/`:
  - `probe.html`: the page, one file of about 30 KB.
  - `README.md`: written for the owner. It covers the five questions, seven
  steps of about five minutes in Edge (Chrome optional), a table of what each
  answer means for the product, what automation tested, and what only the person
  can do. It cites Turn 12 and Turn 6 of the 2026-11-21 record for the owner's
  words.
  - `check.mjs`: `node check.mjs seal` rewrites the CSP hashes after an edit;
  `node scripts/browser-lane.mjs --label <who> -- node check.mjs check
  <scratch>` runs the checks.
  - `probe-desktop.png` and `probe-narrow.png`: the two pictures.

  No product code changed. Memory, the records, `AGENTS.md` and `Work/` are
  untouched. The workspace README says probe pages are "disposable and never
  committed"; this one is committed because the brief asks for it, as
  `git-in-a-page/probe.html` was before it.

  ## The page
  - **Policy and wiring:** `default-src 'none'`, with the inline script and
  style allowed by hash, plus `base-uri 'none'` and `form-action 'none'`. All
  events are attached with listeners.
  - **Header:** says plainly whether it was opened from a file on disk. Small
  raw values underneath: browser and version, secure context, whether each
  picker exists, and whether storage is kept for good.
  - **1. Own window:**
    - Plain answer: tab or own window. Raw values: display-mode, address bar
  visible, and a count of launches in a tab versus in a window of its own.
    - Edge and Chrome shortcut targets of the form `"…msedge.exe"
  --app="file:///…"`, worked out from the page's own address, each with a Copy
  button. A warning appears if a target is longer than 259 characters.
    - A short line on how to tell whether it worked, and a "What you saw" choice
  for the taskbar icon.
  - **2. Folder remembered:**
    - Folder handles are kept in IndexedDB. On each launch the page asks
  `queryPermission`.
    - Answers: "kept your folder from last time and gave it back without
  asking", "asks before giving it back" (with a press that calls
  `requestPermission`), "asked again, and gave the folder back without picking
  it again", or "had forgotten". It also says when the folder is no longer where
  it was, or when the browser refused to keep it.
    - With several folders it reports whether one press gave all of them back or
  each needed its own press. That is the "update all" question.
  - **3. Second copy:** marks the storage with an id, and each copy with its
  path, in both localStorage and IndexedDB. It lists every copy it finds and
  says whether the two stores agree.
  - **4. Listing:** walks the picked folder lazily, folder by folder, showing
  "Listing Files... (n)" as it goes and the time at the end. It reads no file's
  contents. The older folder input is offered too, labelled as including the
  person's own clicks.
  - **5. Saving:** "Save a copy of this page" writes the page through the save
  picker. A Yes/No/Not known choice records whether Windows marked this copy as
  downloaded.
  - **Results:** a few plain lines, with names left out unless the box is
  ticked. A Copy button falls back to the older copy command if the clipboard
  call fails. "Start over" forgets everything the page kept.

  ## What I tested and saw
  All 22 checks held in headless Chromium 149, through the lane:
  - **Opening:** opens from `file://` with no console error, warning or page
  error, and makes no request other than its own file.
  - **Marks:** persist across two launches of one profile.
  - **Second copy:** a copy at another path in the same profile sees the first
  copy's mark, and the two stores agree. A fresh profile sees only itself. So in
  headless Chromium, every file opened from disk shares one storage per
  profile.
  - **Saved copy:** keeps the page's script and style byte-identical and opens
  under its own policy.
  - **Counting:** the older folder input counts a known folder correctly (153
  files in 30 folders). The lazy walk gives the same count and shows "Listing
  Files... (n)" while it runs.
  - **Results:** names are left out until the box is ticked, and the copy
  reports "Copied."
  - **Narrow width:** no sideways scroll at 390 px.

  Three findings came along the way:
  1. Headless Chromium offers `showDirectoryPicker` but aborts it at once
  ("Nothing was picked"), and Playwright cannot intercept it.
  2. The browser's private file system (OPFS) refuses a `file://` page with a
  SecurityError. If that holds in Edge, the product cannot use it from a file on
  disk.
  3. Because of 1 and 2, the lazy walk was tested with an in-memory stand-in
  folder. That stand-in cannot be stored in IndexedDB, so I made the page say so
  honestly ("would not let this page keep the folder") rather than later claim
  the folder was forgotten. The pictures show that state in section 2.

  The save picker was also a stand-in.

  ## Pictures, judged by eye
  Both are at the paths above: desktop at 1280 px and narrow at 390 px.
  - The page reads at a glance: a bold coloured answer per section, small grey
  monospace raw values, and blue buttons.
  - After the first look I made the two shortcut buttons the same width so the
  target boxes line up, and removed a gap left by an empty status line.
  - At narrow width everything stacks into one column with no sideways scroll;
  the long targets wrap.
  - Only the light theme was photographed. The dark theme is defined but not
  looked at.

  ## What only the person can do
  Everything that involves a real folder picker, permission prompt, save dialog,
  shortcut, taskbar, Windows download mark, or a large real folder:
  - whether an app-mode window gets its own taskbar icon;
  - what `queryPermission` says after a real restart, and whether one press
  gives several folders back;
  - whether Edge's storage for files on disk matches headless Chromium's;
  - how long the lazy walk takes over about 100,000 files;
  - whether the save picker works and whether a marked file behaves differently.

  ## Refused commands
  One command was refused by the worktree harness, even though it did not name
  git: a `mkdir && cat > file <<EOF … && cd … && node …` chain that wrote a
  throwaway script into my scratch folder. I wrote the file with the file tool
  and ran it as a plain command instead. Everything else ran.

  The scratch folder `local-app/` has been removed.

› Message from general-purpose
  ## Commit (on the worktree branch, from `8bc5736cd`)
  - `b9a5d1c30`: the C# compiler resolves every documentation comment's `cref`.
  The five samples' kept records are written again, with new cref cases in
  `names` and `estate`, an oracle test holding the compiler's answers, and the
  record with counts, proposal and language table. No reader changed. Nothing
  under `adapters/`, Memory, Work, AGENTS.md or the session records was touched.

  ## What was built
  - `scripts/oracle/csharp-compiler.cs` now uses `DocumentationMode.Parse`. For
  every `XmlCrefAttributeSyntax` it records the file, line, element, the text as
  written, and an outcome: `resolved`, `Ambiguous` or `unresolved`. Each
  resolved symbol carries its kind, display name and declaring files.
  - The code's names are still walked without trivia. Edges, declared names and
  projects came out equal to the old records on all five samples and on DNN
  (24,837) and mojoPortal (8,299).
  - `scripts/oracle/csharp-compare.ts` gains `documentationPairs` (pairs to
  every declaring file, never a namespace, never the file itself, every
  candidate of an ambiguous reference) and `report.documentation` (`pairs`,
  `alsoCode`, `authorOnly`, `drawn`). It is printed by `npm run oracle:csharp`,
  and `--detail` lists the pairs only the author's word makes.
  - New sample files:
    - `names/App/Documented.cs` names one of each kind: a type, a member (on
  `see` and `inheritdoc`), a nested type, `Lib.Helper{T}` beside `Lib.Helper`,
  two overloads in two partial parts, the bare overload name, a namespace, a
  type on `permission`, `Missing`, and `Lib.Thing` from an unreferenced project.
    - `names/App/Runner.cs` and `names/App/Runner.Text.cs` hold the two partial
  parts with one overload each.
    - `estate/Hub/PaymentHub.cs` gains a `seealso` naming another project's
  overload; its code binds that pair too.
  - Tests:
    - `tests/integration/live-docs/csharp-oracle.test.ts` has a `DOCUMENTATION`
  register per sample: every reference with the compiler's answer in words, plus
  `alsoCode`, `authorOnly` and `drawn`.
    - `scripts/oracle/csharp-compare.test.ts` has two stand-in cases.
    - Both were shown red with the namespace rule switched off. The integration
  test only went red after the `permission` reference was moved off
  `App.Other.Gadget`, which had put `Other.cs` among the pairs by another road
  and hidden the rule.

  ## Counts (copies of DNN `9bc6b06f4` and mojoPortal `ecbaf7f40`)
  | | DNN | mojoPortal |
  |---|---:|---:|
  | crefs | 8,453 | 35 |
  | resolved to nothing (includes framework types, since the compilation has no
  framework references) | 2,197 | 6 |
  | ambiguous | 0 | 1 |
  | file pairs named | 1,312 | 4 |
  | of those, code binds too | 1,018 | 4 |
  | author's word only | **294** | 0 |
  | drawn by the adapter today | 982 | 4 |

  - On DNN, 1,191 of the 2,197 unresolved references are written as `System.`,
  `Microsoft.` or a well-known framework name. That is a pattern count, not the
  compiler's.
  - **The 86 pairs the guard removed from DNN were all explicit `cref`s; none
  was a type's name in prose.**
    - 16 resolve to the target and the code binds the pair too.
    - 66 resolve to the target and only the comment makes the pair.
    - 3 sit in whole-file `#if NET_2_0`/`NET_3_5` sections the oracle's symbols
  leave inactive.
    - 1 (`Meta.HttpEquiv`) the compiler resolves to the file itself, while the
  reader had taken the designer part.
  - Correction to the brief: the "3 on mojoPortal" are nopCommerce's ("each a
  `<see cref>`" in the batch record). mojoPortal's C# pairs were unchanged by
  the guard. nopCommerce was not run.
  - MSBuild could not evaluate 34 of DNN's 82 projects and 4 of mojoPortal's 25.
  Each is listed in `projects-msbuild-could-not-evaluate.txt`.

  ## Finding that shapes the proposal
  The Live Docs already carry these references, as text:
  - `csharp.xmldoc.ts` puts each `see`/`seealso` cref under a symbol's `##### …
  — Links` list as backticked text with no link.
  - `exception` goes to `— Exceptions`, `inheritdoc` to `— Additional
  Documentation` as raw markup, and `permission` to `— Unsupported Doc Tags`.

  Two rendering faults showed up, not mended because they are in `adapters/`:
  - The summary writes `Lib.Helper{T}` as `Lib.Helper`, so the generic and the
  plain type read alike.
  - A `<seealso cref/>` written inside a summary vanishes and leaves "; ".

  ## Proposal: three alternatives
  - **A. One more kind of edge.** A `(documentation)` qualifier on a
  Dependencies line and an `EdgeKind` of `"documentation"`. Cheapest to write.
  But every consumer of `edges`, `outbound` and `inbound` (reachability, paths,
  dead code, the board, inspect's counts) must filter it out, and one forgotten
  filter makes a class named only in a comment reachable.
  - **B. The symbol's existing `— Links` list, resolved into real links
  (recommended).** Unresolved references stay as text. The `exception`,
  `inheritdoc` and `permission` references join the same list with their
  element's word. The graph keeps `documentation: GraphEdge[]` apart from
  `edges`, outside `outbound`/`inbound`, with `documentedBy` for the reverse.
  Reachability, paths and dead code stay safe by construction. The map and
  inspect must ask for it explicitly. The format grows least, and the same list
  serves `{@link}` in other languages later.
  - **C. A mark on the target symbol, no file pair.** The smallest change to the
  graph, and traversals need no change. But the relation is not drawn between
  the two things, which is what the owner asked to be honoured.

  I recommend B: "the map does not lie" is the value A would pay.

  ## Language table (in the record, no code)
  - TypeScript/JSDoc: `JSDocLink` nodes resolved with

  Ready for you: the Edge probe page

  The probe page (AI-Agent-Workspace/AgentSessionHistory/2026/11/2026-11-22/Prob
  es/local-app/probe.html) answers the five things I could only answer from
  memory yesterday. Its README (AI-Agent-Workspace/AgentSessionHistory/2026/11/2
  026-11-22/Probes/local-app/README.md) has seven steps, about five minutes in
  Edge.

  1. Does a shortcut that starts Edge in app mode on the local file give a
     window and taskbar icon of its own, with no URL involved?
  2. Is a picked folder given back after a restart, and does one press give back
     several? This is your "update all".
  3. Does a second copy of the file see the first copy's storage?
  4. How long does Edge take to list a large folder?
  5. Does saving a copy work, and does a file Windows marks as downloaded behave
     differently?

  The page fetches nothing and sends nothing, and its results leave folder and
  file names out unless you tick a box. I ran its checks myself: 22 of 22 held.
  I corrected one line of the builder's README, which said you had refused a
  wrapper; the record holds no such word from you.

  The author's word: measured, and a ruling of mine you can reverse

  The C# compiler now answers for every <see cref>, and its answers are
  committed as a test before any reader changes (the record (AI-Agent-Workspace/

  - Yesterday's guard: all 86 pairs it removed from DNN were explicit
    references; none was a name in prose.
  - Found on the way: the Live Docs already hold these references, as unlinked
    text in a list under each symbol.

  My ruling: that list becomes real links, and the graph keeps them apart from
  dependencies. "Is this dead code" and the paths then cannot follow an author's
  word by accident, which is the second value, the map does not lie. The cost
  is a second list for everything that draws lines. If you answer that an
  author's reference should be a plain dependency, merging the two is a small
  change. The reader work starts when the engine's builder hands back, since
  both are in the engine.

  ## The blind reading of the owner's values: finished, one commit

  **I found a value of the owner's that fits none of the five: "Do it the right
  way — every mess is our mess."** All three judge runs made it a value of its
  own, under nearly the same name, with 154 to 179 statements each, about a
  tenth of the whole list. It holds the owner's refusals of "MVP", "prototype"
  and "minimal", "It's only us in this repo. Every mess is our mess" (said
  several times from December 2025 to March 2026), and the November 18 line
  "build the right thing, fully". The nearest of the five is "and no simpler",
  but that phrase sets a limit on simplicity; it does not ask for completeness
  or for owning a mess. The guide states it only as a working rule. 85% of its
  statements are from before September 2026, but it has been restated since.

  **Commit:** `bd9c3ab5a` on `worktree-agent-aeb8d2cdf41fd1fff`, on top of
  `8bc5736cd`. The folder is `AI-Agent-Workspace/AgentSessionHistory/2026/11/202
  6-11-22/Probes/design-philosophy-reading/`:
  - `README.md`: method, coverage, the instructions exactly as given, the
  blindness check, numbers, cost, the judge's results and my comparison.
  - `statements.md`: 1,715 statements, each linking to its message.
  - `clustering-1.md`, `clustering-2.md`, `clustering-3.md`: the judge's three
  answers, unchanged.
  - `extract-owner-messages.mjs` and `blind-reading.mjs`: the scripts.

  No product code and no Memory, record, guide, philosophy or Work file was
  touched. `slopcop:markdown`, `slopcop:symbols`, `slopcop:assets` and
  `docs:links:enforce` are clean. ESLint ignores this folder.

  ### Coverage
  - **Messages extracted:** 3,683 typed by the owner.
    - 643 from the 46 records. Every `## Turn` heading was checked: the only
  turns with nothing extracted are pictures alone or a pasted log alone.

  fenced blocks.

  ### What the judge found (Opus at high effort, three fresh runs)
  The runs gave 13, 14 and 12 values. Pairwise agreement is 0.53 to 0.61: they
  agree on the large shapes and differ at the edges. Eleven values appear in all
  three runs:
  1. Seeing it: "Intuitive to the human eye" / "Keep understanding from running
  away", 361 to 417, the largest in every run.
  2. "As simple as it can be, and no simpler", 186 to 215.
  3. "Do it the right way", 154 to 179.
  4. "Publication-grade rigor", 157 to 169.
  5. "A web of truth" (the documents as the source of truth), 139 to 152.
  6. "Local, nonproprietary, and permanent", 73 to 119.
  7. "What will happen if I make this change?" (the product's promise), 72 to
  175.
  8. Copilot's "visibility and agency" / "pit of success", 63 to 84.
  9. "MDMD is just a convention" / "most portable", 54 to 165.
  10. "Code Like Clay" (docs first, iterate, fail fast), 67 to 78.
  11. "Leverage solved problems" / "Common sense", 38 to 49.

  Three more appear in two runs: "Honest collaboration" / "Mutual respect",
  "Auditability" and "Dogfooding". 21 statements fit no group in any run. Most
  are fragments, but five are about naming, and run 1 says they "nearly form a
  small value of their own". Six pairs that pull against each other are named in
  all three runs.

  ### The comparison (mine), most important first
  1. **"Do it the right way" fits none of the five**, as above.
  2. **"The map does not lie" is the only one of the five that no run makes a
  value of its own.** Its plainest statements ("don't lie to the user", the ugly
  truth) fall inside rigor (runs 1 and 2) or inside the seeing value (run 3).
  Rigor is broader: it covers how anything is known to be right, not only the
  map.
  3. **"It is yours" joins two things every run kept apart.** Local and
  nonproprietary is one value in all three runs. The other half goes elsewhere:
  "silently", "reversible" and "honor their word" land in the seeing,
  portability, rigor or solved-problems values.
  4. **Values with no home among the five, mostly ways of working:** leverage
  solved problems (all three runs), Code Like Clay (the method holds only its
  "competing forces" part), auditability and the web of truth (the web of truth
  is only 5–12% from September onward), and dogfooding.
  5. **The product's promise** ("what will this change impact") is a value to
  the judge. The philosophy leaves it to the vision. 81–89% of its statements
  are from before September.
  6. **Agents:**
     - The Copilot-as-consumer value has a partial home in "meets you where you
  are".
     - The collaboration value has no home among the five. 57–67% of it is from
  September onward, as high as the seeing value's share.
     - The judges used the purpose's own words ("understanding … run away") both
  for the collaboration with the owner (runs 1 and 2) and to name the seeing
  value (run 3).
  7. **Where the five do match:** "A person can see it", "As simple as
  possible", and the local half of "It is yours" match in every run. "Meets you
  where you are" is a value in run 3 only.
  8. **The method holds up:**
     - The owner's "maximize competing interests" statements fall under
  simplicity or Code Like Clay.
     - The pulls include one inside the fifth value: "simplest is most correct"
  against "the simplest solution was not the best".
     - They also include one between the second and fourth, degraded mode
  against compiler oracles, which is the philosophy's own trade.
  9. **Era:** the five match what the owner has said since September 2026. The
  gaps are mostly from the Copilot era, so for each one it is for the owner to
  say whether it still holds.

  ships in `dist`, because the module imports nothing and one build
  configuration was simpler.
  - `.mdmd/layer-3/stored-form.mdmd.md`: the specification, marked a proposal.
  - `AI-Agent-Workspace/AgentSessionHistory/2026/11/2026-11-22/Probes/stored-for
  m-module/README.md`: the record, with `measure.ts` and `scan-of-graph.ts`
  beside it.
  - 9 new Live Docs, each with an authored Purpose and Notes.
  - Edits to the root `package.json` (workspace entry, and the build runs this
  package first), `package-lock.json`, and `vitest.config.ts` (unit include).

  ### What was built
  - **The layout:** an 8-byte signature `89 'F' 'P' 'K' 0D 0A 1A 0A`, then
  frames. Each frame has a 45-byte header (kind, count, raw length, stored
  length, SHA-256 of the raw bytes) and a raw-deflate body.
  - **Frame order:** head, then (strings? records)* in groups of 256 files, then
  paths (front-coded), outbound wires (varint gaps), and an end frame. The end
  frame holds the content's name: the SHA-256 of every frame's hash. The name,
  extension, signature letters and the text's `fact-pack` line are all
  provisional.
  - **Writing as facts arrive:** files go in one at a time in path order.
  Strings are numbered in the order they are first met, and each group's new
  strings are set down just before its records.
  - **Resuming:** `resumeFrom` keeps everything up to the last full group, and
  the resumed write ends in the same bytes.
  - **No Node-only or third-party code:** the type-check runs against the
  browser library with no Node types. Node 26 runs the `.ts` source unchanged by
  type stripping.
  - **Each scan says what it holds and left out:** the head carries a left-out
  list (reason, count, detail). Absolute paths, drives and `file:` links are
  refused wherever a path or link is kept.

  ### Numbers (Node 26.13.1, shared machine at load 7.5–8.9, fresh process per
  read, 3 runs each)
  Peak memory includes about 70 MB for tsx itself.

  | | This repo, 1,294 files | Estate, 41 files | DNN, 10,777 files |
  |---|---|---|---|
  | `index.json` gzipped | 1.38 MB | 10.4 KB | 3.64 MB |
  | Stored form | **1.09 MB** (79 %) | **7.7 KB** (74 %) | **2.17 MB** (60 %) |
  | First answer, stored | 47–73 ms | 32–34 ms | **86–128 ms** |
  | First answer, JSON | 178–238 ms | 2.5–3.1 ms | 717–738 ms |
  | Whole map, stored | 295–362 ms | 50 ms | 0.98–1.18 s |
  | Whole map, JSON | 178–238 ms | 2.5–3.1 ms | 0.72–0.74 s |
  | Peak memory, stored | 109–119 MB | 72–73 MB | **252–255 MB** |
  | Peak memory, JSON | 122–123 MB | 70–72 MB | 393–438 MB |
  | Sections inflate to | 3.3× | 3.2× | 4.1× |

  - "First answer" is what depends on the most depended-on file, by name.
  - **On all three corpora** the graph read back equals `index.json` field by
  field, and printed it is byte for byte that `index.json`. The engine's own
  link rule supplied the targets, so this is an independent check.
  - **Round trips, all three corpora:** binary → text → binary gives the same
  bytes, and text → binary → text gives the same text.
  - **Slower whole read:** the stored form's whole read is slower than parsing
  JSON. It checks every hash, every string's numbering, every path and every
  wire. Building refusal messages lazily cut this repo's whole read from 450–590
  ms to 295–362 ms. I did not profile further.
  - **Tiny scans:** the stored form loses on the estate sample (32 ms against 3
  ms), because streams and hashing cost a fixed start.

  ### What each test proves; the fuzzer was seen to fail first
  - **Unit cases (9):**
    - every fact is read back;
    - writing as facts arrive, the pieces handed on, and writing all at once
  give the same bytes;
    - text converts both ways without loss;
    - one text per set of facts: nine non-canonical variants are refused;
    - edges land only on scanned files;
    - resuming from four cut points gives the same bytes;
    - the writer refuses absolute paths, `..`, lone surrogates and `file:`
  links, and goes on with the next file;
    - re-framed files that are valid in shape but not canonical are refused: a
  string twice, a string never used, an overlong varint, lying wires, trailing
  bytes;
    - the module's source holds no import.
  - **Fuzzer:** 800 seeded mutants of two valid files, in eight kinds: bit
  flips, cuts, lying counts and lengths, bytes put in or taken out, frames
  doubled or swapped, noise, and a 256 MiB raw-deflate bomb. Each is read in a
  worker that is stopped after 10 s without an answer; memory is bounded at 48
  MiB plus 64 × the input size.
    - Result: 790 refused, 10 identical files read as facts, no crash, no hang.
  The most any read held was 12 MB, a quarter of its bound. The case takes 22 s.
  - **Red first:** I ran the same case once against `careless.mjs`, a reader
  that trusts declared sizes. It failed: it hung at seed 67 (68 answers of 800),
  crashed on cuts and noise, held 1.56 GB on the bombs, and returned facts for
  six mutants the module refuses.
  - **Standing proof the fuzzer can fail:** a second case holds that the fuzzer
  catches the careless reader in at least two ways.
  - **Gates, all green:** `npm run lint`, `npm run build`, the package's 11
  tests, `slopcop:markdown`, `slopcop:assets`, `slopcop:symbols`,
  `docs:links:enforce`.
  - I did not run the repo's whole unit suite or `safe:commit`.

  ### The history's cache key does not move
  `lockIdentity` gives `697191d3f844372b` before and after the lock changed. The
  code half stays `5b1c8f445d32d867`, and `modified` stays null. Neither the
  engine nor the generator reaches the new lock entries. The package's
  `tsconfig.json` does not extend `tsconfig.base.json`, which is keyed and was
  left untouched. The history was not rebuilt.

  ### Forks I decided
  - **The content is canonical, the compressed bytes are not.** The reader
  checks the uncompressed sections and the name is over them. Recompressing on
  every read would cost a write's time and tie the format to one zlib's exact
  output.
  - **Links carry what they name (target and anchor), not whether that file was
  scanned.** Later files are unknown when a file is written, so the graph
  decides landing at the end. This carries one fact more than `index.json`: what
  a dangling link names.
  - **Only outbound wires are stored.** Inbound is derived in about 1 ms at
  DNN's size.
  - **A file-level inflation bound of 64 × file + 1 MiB.** Real scans inflate
  3.2–4.1×. Paths are bounded by the strings' length, and the text export by the
  same bound, because one stored name can be repeated millions of times.
  - **A refused file leaves the writer usable.**

  ### Forks left to you
  - **SCIP**, read by one general-purpose subagent of my tier (with LSIF and
  Kythe for contrast):
    - SCIP calls itself a "transmission format", "not meant as a storage
  format".
    - File dependencies appear only as positioned occurrences, which we do not
  make.
    - Protobuf is "not (and cannot be) canonical".
    - It has no slot for entries, authored text, cited-by, layer, archetype or
  qualifiers.
    - Its `project_root` is an absolute path.
    - Advice: don't adopt it; perhaps borrow its symbol-string grammar for
  stable symbol names, and add a lossy export later.
  - **Stable names** (spec section 10): a file is its path; a symbol is its path
  and slug; a scan is its content name, which names a moment, not a folder. A
  rename makes a ghost.
  - **The link rule:** a hostile file can claim a link names any file. Checking
  that would need a second copy of the engine's `linkTarget`.
  - **Quick open against whole read:** canonical form is enforced in full only
  by a whole read. A quick open checks only what it touched, which is what buys
  a first answer in about a tenth of the whole read's time.
  - **`graphOf` repeats the engine's edge listing:** measured equal on three
  corpora. One of them goes at wiring.
  - **The provisional name, extension, signature and text first line.**

  ### Unfinished
  - The browser tab through the lane.
  - Five times DNN.
  - Timing a write as facts arrive from disk, with its peak.
  - Profiling the whole read.
  - The configuration-values audit.
  - Which left-out reasons the scanner reports.

  ### Harness refusals and cleanup
  - The harness refused two commands as "too complex to verify": a heredoc
  creating a script, and an inline Python script. Neither named git. I rewrote
  each as a file written with the file tools and ran it with a plain command.
  - My scratch folder `stored-form/` is removed, including the corpus copies. To
  continue, DNN's index must be regenerated, about 5 minutes.

  ## Scan Growth trial: first milestone (built, looked at, committed; some
  checks not finished)

  **Commit** (branch `worktree-agent-abfcd17682e3c7e13`, on top of `8bc5736cd`):
  - `a959e1d20` Add a trial that fills the 3D Local Map as a scan would, to be
  judged by eye before any scanner is changed

  Worktree:
  `/workspaces/Live-Documentation/.claude/worktrees/agent-abfcd17682e3c7e13`. No
  engine or generator file changed.

  ### What was built
  - Two levers in Tuning > 3D Layout > Discovery, both off by default: **Scan
  Growth** (Off / Path order / First-commit order) and **Scan Pace** (As
  measured / Four times slower). They travel in a link only when not default.
  - With Scan Growth on, opening the 3D map with nothing asked plays a scan: no
  file through the listing and the first pass, then files arrive until the
  picture is the one off shows. Choosing the lever or pace again replays it.
  Leaving 3D, opening History or turning it off ends it at the whole picture.
  - Growth is the existing membership change fed in an order: the view gets one
  new entry, `setArrived(graph | null)`, and `render` reads `moment?.graph ??
  arriving ?? graphData`. No second mechanism.
  - The sign at the map's top edge: "Listing Files… (n)" then "Reading File
  Text… (n/m)". Nothing else added. The sidebar's counts follow what the picture
  holds.
  - First-commit order is disabled where the bundle has no history, with the
  reason in the lever's tooltip and description.
  - While a scan plays, the building rule is suspended (hover, press, drag all
  live). Design system rule changed in the same commit, with the owner's words
  and what was seen.
  - Three things in the view apply only while a cut is held: building suspended;
  a group's new seed is born where the group stood; a replaced body's hull is
  built in the same render.

  ### What I saw with my own eye (this repository, 1,297 files)
  - **It looks alive, not stupid.** The two orders look different.
  - **Path order**: one bubble swells in one place for two thirds of the scan,
  with lone files appearing around it; then the test fixtures bring about forty
  small bubbles in a few seconds. Calm, slightly dull. It does **not** read as
  one folder at a time in Connections, because groups are drawn by use, not
  folder.
  - **First-commit order**: small bubbles all over from the first second,
  growing and joining; the main body forms from two neighbours. This is the one
  that looks like the structure "coming in quite nice". More churn.
  - **Camera**: frames the whole growing picture and backs away gently until a
  hand takes it; after an orbit or zoom it stays put for the rest of the scan.
  - **What goes wrong when exploring**: things move under the pointer. Framed
  whole it reads as slow breathing. Zoomed in, a named sphere slid some 180 px
  between two frames four seconds apart, and a group filled the view in one
  frame and was gone in the next. A press still pins the sphere pressed and a
  rest still names it; what a person loses is their place, not their click.
  - **Wrappers**: a bubble grows smoothly. Two that join do not fuse: one stops
  being drawn and the other is larger at the next step. No frame I kept catches
  a join in the act, so that is from the code plus the strips, not directly
  seen.
  - **The veil**: near the end the main body passes the 1,024-file budget and
  turns in one step from a full bubble into a blank grey ball (your 2026-11-21
  finding, met in motion).
  - **The first pass is the dead part**: 5 s (20 s at the slower pace) of
  "(0/1,297)" over an empty map.
  - **Buffering was needed.** The first build fed a cut every frame and the page
  fell behind by about ten seconds. The play now lets arrivals use at most a
  quarter of the page's time (`FEED_SHARE`). After that, at the measured pace in
  path order: median frame 22 to 24 ms through the arrivals, eleven frames over
  100 ms, worst 594 ms. The picture can lag the sign's count by a couple of
  hundred files. The cause of the long frames was not profiled; my guess is the
  main body being packed again whole.

  ### Stumbles in the command-line arm
  - **estate-trace-cli-2, partly.**
    - **What it ran:** `live-docs --help`, `inspect --help`, `visualize --help`,
  then `cat README.md board.md`.
    - **What the tool printed:** only its help.
    - **What it got wrong:** it answered from the README's diagram and never
  named HubProxy, ServiceRouting or PaymentsContext.
    - **Cause: the tool had the fact and the agent did not find the command.**
  Nothing in the help says a path between two files is how a flow is traced.
    - **Second cause:** had it asked, **the output would have misled**. The
  shortest path from Default.aspx to dbo.Payment.sql skips the hub's code, its
  routing, the data context and the stored procedure. It runs through
  GatewaySettings, Gateway/Web.config and Hub/App.config, then PaymentService.cs
  → Payment.cs. This is the 2026-10-27 lesson again: a shortest dependency path
  is not a call sequence.
  - **No command-line answer was wrong, and none rested on a fact the tool
  lacked.** These tasks did not ask anything in the table's no-command gaps.

  ### Forks I decided, each yours to reverse
  - The command-line arm's folder has its Live Docs generated in advance, as a
  repository that uses the tool would hold them. A cold arm, where the agent
  must find `generate` itself, is untried.
  - That arm's system prompt adds one sentence: "The command `live-docs` is
  installed." Without it the two arms would be the same.
  - I used all 8 remaining runs as second runs of the four tasks where the tool
  was queried or the arms might differ, not on new questions. The brief allowed
  only three new ones.
  - The repository copy leaves out all of `.mdmd/` except the board its
  configuration names. In the command-line arm the tool regenerates layer-4, so
  there it has docs with placeholder Purposes.

  ### Fork left to you: how the table stays true
  The document lays out three forms; I recommend the first.
  1. **A ledger and a test (recommended).** A committed `parity.json` with one
  entry per row: the command over the estate, plus a fact its output must
  contain, or the gap's cause. An integration test fails in three cases:
     - an equivalent stops answering;
     - a listed gap starts answering, so the row moves to "yes" in the same
  commit;
     - the page's template gains a control that no row and no by-nature line
  names.

     The document is then printed from the ledger. Cost: about 25 CLI calls, 40
  to 70 s of the integration suite, and one entry per new control. The hard part
  is deciding what counts as a control.
  2. **The table alone, reviewed by hand.** Nothing now; it rots at the first
  new control.
  3. **Blind agents as the check.** Measures what matters, but costs minutes and
  money and cannot run in the gate.

  The stored form exists as a module

  It is a package of its own that imports nothing, with a specification
  (.mdmd/layer-3/stored-form.mdmd.md) written so someone else could build a
  second reader from the text alone. Nothing in the product uses it yet.

  - "Ironclad", tested: a fuzzer feeds the reader 800 damaged files, including a
    compressed section claiming a quarter of a gigabyte. The reader refused
    790, read the 10 that were in fact undamaged, and never hung or crashed. A
    deliberately careless reader given the same files hung, crashed and held
    1.56 GB, which shows the test can fail.
  - Size: 60 to 79 % of today's gzipped index.
  - Speed on DNN (10,777 files): "what depends on this file" answers in about
    0.1 s, against 0.7 s to parse the JSON, at 250 MB against about 400.
  - Where it loses: reading the whole map with every check is slower than
    parsing JSON (about 1.1 s against 0.7 s), and on the 41-file estate it is 32
    ms against 3. The builder is profiling the whole read next, since that is
    the number your "prove it by the efficiency gains" bar still catches.
  - One change I ordered: the format sorted paths the way JavaScript compares
    strings; it will sort by plain UTF-8 bytes instead, so a reader in any
    language agrees. It costs almost nothing now and cannot be changed after a
    first release.
  - Next: a second reader written from the specification alone by a reader who
    has not seen the code, and an independent adversarial review.

  I ran its build, lint and 11 tests myself. I did not verify that adding the
  package leaves the history cache key alone; the builder measured that it does.

  Parity: how far "each gets everything" is true today

  The table (.mdmd/layer-2/parity.mdmd.md) lists 31 things a person can do in
  the page. 17 have a command-line equivalent that was run and seen to answer;
  14 do not.

  - No command at all (7): most-used files, unreferenced symbols, a folder's
    contents and connections, the pinned set, search, a step's authors, a link
    into the Explorer.
  - A command that lacks a part (7): among them, a path from or to a folder,
    which the page already takes.
  - Nothing prompts or touches the network. That was measured, not assumed.
  - Two faults against our own rules: one command reads .mdmd/layer-4 regardless
    of configuration, and the linter's warnings come out in a different order
    each run.
  - A cold scan stamps the clock time into every doc, so the same input does not
    give the same bytes.
  - No installable command exists. That is the delivery shape, which is yours to
    decide.

  ## Milestone 1 (steps 2 and 3 of section 6): done and committed, at 50 minutes

  Branch `worktree-agent-ac72d8a99b54c2e5d`, on top of `8bc5736cd`:
  - `60b01e695`: Take a file's workspace path directly, without a file URL, so
  the engine in a page needs no node:url (step 2)
  - `27b2bf9de`: Read each file once in a scan, through the host that runs the
  engine (step 3)

  I have not started step 4. Steps 5 and 6 are untouched, as you asked.

  ### Step 2
  - **What changed:** the generator was the only caller of the URL round trip.
  It now takes the relative path directly
  (`toWorkspaceRelativePath(absolutePath, root)`), and `toWorkspaceFileUri` is
  deleted.
  - **Stand-in deleted:** `stand-ins/url.mjs`, with its Live Doc.
  - **`node-needs.json`:** loses `node:url` (`fileURLToPath`, `pathToFileURL`)
  from `pathUtils.ts` and from the stand-ins, plus `pathUtils.ts`'s
  `path.isAbsolute`.
  - **Facts:** no doc or index changed apart from the docs of the edited files.

  ### Step 3: what was built
  - **The host interface:** `FileHost` = `read(path)` and `head(path, n)`, in
  the new pure module `tooling/workspaceFiles.ts`.
  - **The Node host:** the new `tooling/nodeHost.ts`, the only module that reads
  a source file from disk.
  - **The page host:** `PAGE_HOST` in `tests/page-scan/page-entry.mjs`. It reads
  through the tree with each read counted, and is passed to the generator as
  the new option `host`.
  - **The run's reads:** `workspaceReads(host, BINARY_SNIFF_BYTES)` keeps, for
  each file read whole, its reading (decoded once) and its first 8,000 bytes.
  - **A failed read is not cached.** The next asker reads again, which keeps
  failure behaviour exactly as it was. Caching failures broke the generator's
  test "a file whose reading throws in the index pass alone", because a passing
  EMFILE then also failed the barrel that re-exports the file.

  **Fork decided: the reads ride on the file index (`WorkspaceFileIndex &
  Partial<WorkspaceReads>`), not on a separate object.**
  - Every reader already receives the index, and every per-run table is already
  a `WeakMap` keyed by it. A second object would mean two run identities and a
  new parameter through every signature.
  - Readers call `readSourceText(path, fileIndex)`. Keeping that call name keeps
  the structural catch test seeing every read.
  - I also added `readingOf` and `headOf` to that test's list of reads, so the
  guard now sees them too.

  **Section 5 readers:** all of them take bytes through the same `read`, with no
  second copy of their logic. Each change is one argument, threaded to the
  helper that reads:
  - the `fileFacts` tables of C#, Java, Go and Rust; C's headers; Python's
  re-export following
  - a project's reach (`dotnetProjects.ts`) and ASP.NET's web applications
  - PowerShell's `buildWorkspace`, Go's walk up to `go.mod`, the Cargo and
  pyproject TOML reads
  - the DOM heuristics, TypeScript's `export *` walk (`dependencies.ts`), and
  the index pass

  C# changes are 5 lines, all lines that obtain bytes, so the other builder's
  merge should be clean.

  **TypeScript's host:** `compilerPaths` is now async and reads ahead in rounds.
  TypeScript is asked to read with what has been read so far, the files it
  asked for are read through the run's reads, and it is asked again until it
  asks for nothing new. That is three rounds for a tsconfig that extends one
  other, and none of TypeScript's rules for finding what a tsconfig extends is
  copied. Its `fileExists` still uses `statSync`, which is step 4's.

  ### Step 3: numbers
  - **`node-needs.json`:**
    - lost `readFileSync` (`json.ts`) and `fs/promises` `open` (generator)
    - `sourceText.ts`'s `promises.readFile` moved to `nodeHost.ts`, which also
  names `promises.open`
    - the stand-ins lost `readFileSync` (`fs.mjs`) and `open`
  (`fs-promises.mjs`)
  - **Reads per file on DNN's Library under Node (1,414 files):** 7,054 reads
  before (1,410 files read five times), 1,414 after (each file once). Output
  digest identical (`02fee93084cbd80e`).
  - **Time and peak memory on DNN's Library:** no change beyond noise. The
  machine was loaded (load average 7–10) and the disk cache warm:

  | | Time (s) | Peak memory (MB) |
  |---|---|---|
  | Before (three runs) | 14.9, 22.7, 23.7 | 508–527 |
  | After (three runs) | 21.1, 22.1, 19.7 | 517–531 |

  - **Page check:** all 50 folders pass with an empty ledger (19 fixtures in
  54.8 s, 31 programs in 1.9 min). In the page, every source file is read once;
  `go.mod` in go/depot is now read once, where it was read 30 times. The only
  files read twice are 5 docs that typescript/tidepool reads back, and that is
  the sink's business.

  ### Verified
  - `npm run lint` and `npm run build` pass.
  - Unit tests: 237 files, 3,606 tests pass. Integration tests: 38 files, 500
  tests pass.
  - The three slopcop audits, `docs:links:enforce` and `live-docs:lint` (1,296

  **One reader test was touched.** `json.test.ts` "throws a failure of the

  the reader no longer calls. The mock now injects the same EMFILE into

  unchanged. Every other reader test is untouched.

  ### Where building showed the design wrong (corrected in it)
  1. **Step 3 does not delete `readFile` from the stand-ins.** Four modules
  still name it, each for another step's reason:
     - the Node host, which readers called without a run (their own tests) read
  through, so it stays in the page's bundle although the page's scan never calls
  it
     - discovery's root `.gitignore` read (step 5)
     - the docs the generator and `graphFiles.ts` read back (step 6)
     - the generator's read of Markdown documents for `Cited By`: Node decodes
  them as `utf8` and keeps a byte-order mark, which `read`'s one rule would
  strip, so I left it alone
  2. **The fork about caches kept by time of change rests on a false premise.**
  Since 2026-11-20, no cache in the C#, C, Go, Java, Python or Rust readers
  outlives its run (`beginRun` empties it).

  ### Forks for you
  - **The time-of-change caches, before step 4.** Either drop the time and key
  facts by the run, which keeps exactly what is kept today. Or keep facts across
  runs by a fingerprint of the bytes, which is new and would spare history
  workers from reparsing files a change did not touch. I have no numbers on that
  second option yet; measuring it would be the first thing in step 4.
  - **Probes answered from the listing in step 4.** In principle this is a
  change of facts: a reader could no longer reach a left-out file. I counted
  every stat/access/readdir probe of a scan by what a listing could answer:

  | Corpus | Probes | Readers' probes of an on-disk file outside the listing |
  |---|---|---|
  | DNN Library | 4,663 | 0 |
  | All 31 sample programs as one folder | 2,700 | 0 (the 8 such probes were all
  discovery's `gitIgnored.ts` and glob) |
  | All fixtures as one folder | 1,147 | 0 (the 10 such probes were all
  discovery's) |

    About 60 probes per scan reach outside the workspace: the grammars, and the
  generator's `scansAbove` check of the folders above. A page cannot answer
  those at all.
  - **Whether to commit the measuring scripts.** `measure.mts` (time, peak
  memory, reads per file, output digest) and `probes.mts` are still in my
  scratch folder (`/tmp/claude-1000/-workspaces-Live-Documentation/8edd198a-ddfc
  -4db4-98d3-afb50c64b8a7/scratchpad/engine-hosts/`). I removed the corpus

  The engine reads each file once

  - Before: in one scan a file was read three to five times, and one sample's
    go.mod thirty times, each reader opening the disk for itself.
  - Now: a host answers two questions about a file, its bytes and its first
    bytes. Under Node the host is the disk; in the page it is the folder you
    picked. The run keeps what each read decoded to, and every reader takes it
    from there.
  - Measured by the builder on DNN's Library (1,414 files): 7,054 reads before,
    1,414 after, with the output identical. Time and memory did not change
    within the noise of a loaded machine. The page check passed on all 50 sample
    folders with nothing in its ledger of differences.

    not do.

  built the merged branch. I ran the tooling tests: 503 of 504 passed. The one
  failure is in a file this merge does not touch, hit its time limit, and passed
  30 of 30 alone. I have not run the page check in the browser on the merged
  state; that waits for a quiet machine.

  Two things I asked the builder to add:
  - A reader that forgets to pass the run's reads silently falls back to the
    disk. "Read once" becomes an assertion of the page check, so that cannot
    happen unseen.
  - The run now holds every file's text until it ends. The design itself says
    this may lose on the weakest machine, so peak memory gets measured on the
    whole of DNN (10,777 files), under Node and in the page.

  My rulings on its forks:
  - A file your list leaves out is invisible to every reader, on principle as
    well as on the builder's count that no reader reaches such a file in three
    corpora.
  - Caches are keyed by the run, which keeps exactly today's behaviour.

  ## Handback: the hand reading of 222 PowerShell wires is now a test

  **Commit**, on the worktree branch, from `b3fe1749a`:
  - `4bbba11e7` Keep the hand reading of 222 PowerShell wires as a test the
  reader can fail

  **The count.** Of the 222 wires, the reader on this branch draws **211** the
  way the judges read them. No false wire of the 81 is drawn. The other 11:
  - **6 true wires it does not draw.** These are held in a register by cause,
  like the C# oracle's `MEASURED_MISSES`:
    - #50, #53, #55 and #71: `OutputWarning` / `OutputError` are defined in both
  `Actions/.Modules/DebugLogHelper.psm1` and
  `Actions/Troubleshooting/Troubleshooting.ps1`, and the calling file's text
  loads neither copy. #71 is `Troubleshoot.Secrets.ps1`, which
  `Troubleshooting.ps1` dot-sources after defining its own copy.
    - #105 and #107: dbatools' `fleet-controller.Tests.ps1` imports `FleetCore`
  through `$script:ControllerRoot`, a path the text doesn't fix, and the two
  names are also defined in `reconcile-runner-fleet.ps1`.
    - **Cause, all six:** the rule as designed. When a name has two copies and
  the caller loads neither, the rule draws nothing. The judges are right in each
  case, and the reader is not at fault; this is where the rule stops (the Nov
  19 probe's "What R does not see").
  - **4 arguable rows, pinned** to what the reader draws today:
    - #11 and #13 are drawn. `AL-Go-TestRepoHelper.ps1` dot-sources
  `AL-Go-Helper.ps1` with `Join-Path`, and that file imports
  `DebugLogHelper.psm1`.
    - #12 and #14 are not drawn.
    - This is the reader improving on the rule as judged on Nov 19, when
  `Join-Path` loads weren't read yet. The judges had left these rows open.
  - **1 unscorable row:** #215, the cold run's shop, which was never committed.
  It is kept in the extract and named in the test.
  - **One reader fault the skeleton can't show:** #60, AL-Go's
  `CommentPublishing.ps1` calling `Test-GlobMatch`.
    - On AL-Go's own text the reader misses it, because the grammar reads
  `Invoke-CopilotPRReview.psm1` with an error at its loop load (line 696). That
  is the known miss from Nov 19 and 21.
    - The skeleton writes that load plainly, so there the wire is drawn and the
  test passes it.
    - It is named in the test's header and in the record, not in the register.

  **pwsh, asked:** I ran small files of my own, written in the shapes of #71 and
  #50, with `pwsh -File`. They are kept in `pwsh-probe/` beside the record.
  - **#71's shape:** the call reached the copy in `Troubleshooting.ps1`, as the
  judges said.
  - **#50's shape:** the module's call reached `DebugLogHelper.psm1`, as the
  judges said.
  - **A third run shows #50 is close:** a module's call also reaches a function
  defined by the script that is running. So #50's true verdict depends on the
  judges' other claim, that the function holding line 677, `GetLatestRelease`,
  never runs under `Troubleshooting.ps1`. By the parser's record nothing there
  calls it by name. A call through some other function was not traced.

  **The form I chose: the skeleton, built at test time from a committed
  extract.**
  - **What is committed:**
  `tests/integration/fixtures/powershell-judged/extract.json`. It holds 210
  files (dbatools 138, AL-Go 59, AzureStack-Tools 6, tSQLt 7) and all 222 rows
  with both verdicts, 135,182 bytes, plus `licences/*.txt` (4 files, about 12.7
  KB).
  - **What the test does:**
  `tests/integration/live-docs/powershell-judged.test.ts` writes each corpus's
  skeleton into a temp folder and runs the real `powershellAdapter.analyze` over
  every calling file, with the skeleton's files as its index.
  - **Skeleton lines:** `function X { }` at the file's own level, `& { function
  X { } }` where the original is local to a function or block, the judged calls
  as written, and loads rewritten plainly as `. / Import-Module / &
  "$PSScriptRoot/…"`. Manifests carry only their file entries. A test checks
  that every skeleton line is one of these forms.
  - **Why not a recorded table:** the reader has no exported pure function for
  the rule (`calledFiles` takes its own internal workspace). A table would end
  up grading a rebuild of the reader inside the test.
  - **What the skeleton grades:** the grammar's reading of definitions and
  whether each sits at the file's own level, call names, aliases and case, loads
  by `$PSScriptRoot`, modules and how far imports reach, test archetype by
  path, and the list of names PowerShell ships.
  - **What it doesn't grade:** how the corpora actually write their loads
  (`Join-Path`, variables, loops), since keeping that would mean keeping their
  code.

  **Fidelity, measured.** I read every row four ways. The table is
  `crosscheck.tsv` beside the record.

  | Reading | Agrees with the reader on the skeleton |
  | --- | --- |
  | the rule over the parser's facts, on each whole corpus | 221 of 221 |
  | the rule over the parser's record of the skeleton | 221 of 221 |
  | the reader on each corpus's real text | 220 of 221 (only #60 differs) |

  **Seen red, then green:**
  - With two verdicts flipped (#2 and #4), the row check against `samples.md`

  **Commit:** `f9d4ea1ab` "Order the stored form's paths by UTF-8 bytes, state
  its text form exactly, and read it whole in about JSON's time". It is on
  `worktree-agent-a5522d16dedb937a3`, on top of `1e5858e11`, and is ready for
  you to merge. The record is appended under "Second milestone, 2026-11-22" in
  `AI-Agent-Workspace/AgentSessionHistory/2026/11/2026-11-22/Probes/stored-form-
  module/README.md`. Three probe scripts sit beside it: `stream.ts`,
  `read-time.mjs` and `index-of.mjs`.

  **Gates:**
  - `npm run lint`, `npm run build`, `docs:links:enforce`, and the three slopcop
  audits (markdown, assets, symbols) all pass.
  - The package's 18 cases and its fuzzer pass.
  - The Live Docs are regenerated. The moved files' docs were moved with them,
  so their authored Purposes were kept.
  - `npm run test:unit`, run once over the whole unit project: 3,619 of 3,620
  cases pass.
    - The one failure is `generatorIdentity.test.ts`, the case "names as roots
  every package the two packages load without declaring it". It timed out at 5.3
  s, and takes 8.2 s run alone at a load of 15.
    - It reads only the engine's and generator's sources, which I did not touch.
  The config already records it as "1.7 s alone, past five seconds at a load of
  20 to 40".
    - I left its deadline alone, since `packages/generator` is outside my brief.
  - The machine was at a load of 15 to 47 throughout, against 8 in the first
  milestone. Every time below is inflated and is compared only with runs made
  beside it, interleaved.

  ### Ruling 5: path order is UTF-8 byte order
  - **What changed:** `comparePaths` is exported. It compares UTF-16 units,
  except that at the first unit that differs, a surrogate sorts after any other
  unit. That gives code point order, which equals UTF-8 byte order. Section 2.3
  of the spec is rewritten. Section 7.1 now says the engine's sink will sort
  with this comparator when the two are wired, so there is one comparator.
  `scan-of-graph.ts` sorts with it.
  - **Red first:** three cases were written before the change and all three
  failed (for example "expected 1 to be -1"):
    - `dir/\u{E000}.ts` must sort before `dir/\u{1F600}.ts`;
    - 20,000 random pairs drawn from every code-point range, each held to
  `Buffer.compare` of the two UTF-8 encodings;
    - the writer, the reader and the text form must keep that order and refuse
  the reverse.
  - **The three corpora:** none holds a path with a character from U+E000 up (0
  of 1,303, 0 of 41, 0 of 10,777). So nothing reorders. Measured again after the
  change on all three, `graphOf` still prints byte for byte as each
  `index.json`, and both round trips are exact.

  ### Ruling 6: the text form's bytes are stated by the spec
  - **What changed:** new section 8.1 gives every form:
    - the seven short escapes;
    - `\u00` plus two lowercase hex digits for the other controls up to U+001F;
    - everything else written literally in UTF-8, named one by one: `/`, U+007F,
  the C1 controls, U+2028, U+2029, U+FEFF, and code points above U+FFFF (never
  as surrogate escapes);
    - numbers in decimal: no sign, point, exponent or leading zero;
    - no whitespace between tokens;
    - UTF-8 without a byte-order mark, LF line endings.

    `scanOfText` and `bytesOfText` now also accept bytes, decoded strictly.
  - **Four cases**, each expectation written by hand from the rule:
    - 21 characters, each written as the rule says;
    - three numbers;
    - eleven other ways of writing the same text, each refused;
    - a byte-order mark, a `FF` byte and CR LF, each refused.
  - **Red first:** with the writer temporarily swapped for one that escapes
  U+2028 and writes uppercase hex, two of the four cases failed. I then restored
  it.

  **Other places the spec leaned on JavaScript, found and rewritten:**
  - **Strings (§2.2):** they were defined as having "no lone surrogate", a
  UTF-16 idea. They are now sequences of Unicode scalar values, decoded strictly
  by Unicode table 3-7. A byte-order mark inside a string is part of it, and
  two strings are equal when their bytes are, with no normalization.
  - **Path and link checks (§2.4):** they leaned on regular expressions. They
  are now stated by ASCII code point, with ASCII case-folding only for `file:`.
  The code now checks them in one pass.
  - **The text export's size bound:** it counted UTF-16 units; it now counts
  UTF-8 bytes.
  - **The graph (§5):** it said "exactly those of `deriveLiveDocGraph`"; the
  text is now the authority.
    - `toSymbol` comes only with `to` and an anchor.
    - "Holds `type-only`" means one qualifier exactly equal to it.
    - `basis` is the first qualifier, in order, that matches.
    - Outbound and inbound are in byte order.
    - The order of an object's members is not a fact.
  - **Limits (§3.5):** MiB is given in bytes, and the inflation total is stated
  as the sum of every frame's raw length, the end's included.
  - **Counts (§2.1):** the spec now names the early refusals a reader may make
  and says none of them changes which files are accepted.
  - **Document path (§4.1):** stated as a join rather than as a call to
  `docPathOf`.

  ### Ruling 7: `testScans.ts` is out of what ships
  I chose a test folder. The tests, the fuzzer and `testScans.ts` moved to
  `packages/stored-form/test/`, so `src/` holds only the module. The build
  includes `src/` alone, so `dist/` holds only `storedForm.js` and its
  declarations. The unit include now points at
  `packages/stored-form/test/**/*.test.ts`.

  Both of the package's suites now run in `unit-serial`, which is the repo's
  precedent for heavy suites. The resume case took 4.3 s at load 39 and 6.0 s
  when run beside the fuzzer, against the 5 s deadline. No case was trimmed and
  no deadline raised.

  ### Numbers
  **Written as facts arrive, from disk** (one line per file, each piece appended
  as it is set down):
  - DNN: 2.8–3.8 s, peak 194–203 MB (about 70 MB of that is tsx). The bytes are
  identical to writing all at once.
  - Five times DNN: 9.4 s, 254 MB.
  - For comparison, the research's streaming press measured 2.3 s / 188 MB at
  DNN and 7.5 s / 255 MB at five times.

  **Whole read, profiled on DNN, under bare Node running the TypeScript
  source:**
  - Before any change, self time went to: decoding strings one call at a time
  (14 %), checking paths, with a split and two regexes on every target (11 %),
  idle while inflates and hashes ran one after another (11 %), and garbage
  collection (7.5 %).
  - Three changes, none of which drops a check:
    1. Every section is inflated and hashed at once, before decoding in order.
    2. Each distinct string is checked as a path or link once, by its number.
    3. Each strings section is decoded in one strict call. A string that begins
  with a continuation byte is refused, so no character can cross from one string
  into the next. A new case shows a character split across two strings (`C3` |
  `A9`) refused, and a `FF` byte refused. Paths are built in one reused buffer.
  - A first try, a fast path for short ASCII strings, was slower and was
  replaced.

  | DNN, interleaved runs at load ~33 | First answer | Facts read and checked |

  - Reading and checking the facts now takes about as long as JSON, or less.
  - Building the graph adds about 0.35 s, so the whole map with the graph is
  15–20 % slower than parsing JSON that already contains it.
  - Most of what is left is the check that no string is stored twice: about 120
  ms for 109,000 strings, measured alone. That check is the price of one form
  per set of facts.
  - Peak memory rose 10–65 MB because all sections' raw bytes are now held at
  once until each is read. It is still about two thirds of JSON's.
  - Not tried: building the graph only when a consumer asks for it.

  First milestone, handed back at 02:26 UTC (about 43 minutes in). The product,
  tests, rule documents and evidence are in one commit; the browser verification
  is incomplete and two cases have not been seen passing since their fix.

  ## Commit
  - `3b36ba6e0` on `worktree-agent-ad47c0821418d756a` (base `b3fe1749a`),
  worktree
  `/workspaces/Live-Documentation/.claude/worktrees/agent-ad47c0821418d756a`,
  clean: "Make the pull-back the 2D arrival's default, waiting on the reader,
  and let a wire's words be entered".

  ## What was built
  1. **Arrival** defaults to Reading, Then Neighbours, Look After. The other
  forms stay as lever values, and `ARRIVALS[0]` is the default as the link
  convention expects.
  2. **Arrival Waits** gains `attended`, labelled "While Pointed At Or Focused",
  and it is the default.
     - After the rest, the ease begins at the first frame where neither a
  pointer brought to the card nor `:focus-visible` inside the card holds it.
     - The old trial stays as "While Pointed At, Then A Rest More"; Never stays.
     - New lever **Arrival Rest**, 0.5 to 6 s, default 1.5, carried by a link.
     - Pure rules are in `camera-intent.ts` (`arrivalReleased`,
  `arrivalRestStartsAgain`, `arrivalEndedBy`, `ARRIVAL_REST_S`), with unit
  tests.
     - Reduced motion lands on the neighbours at once, as before.
  3. **Wire Answers** defaults to On, and its panel meets the three words.
     - Hoverable: the panel takes the pointer, and the words stay inside the
  convex shape of the asked point and the panel, widened 10 px (`onTheWay` in
  `pointerRest.ts`, unit-tested). Text selects and copies; a press in the panel
  neither pans nor pins.
     - Dismissible: Escape takes a rested pointer's words away, and they stay
  away until the pointer has been elsewhere.
     - Persistent: no clock removes them.
     - Direction Marks is untouched.
  4. **A cost found by the measurement and mended.** With Wire Answers on,
  `--wire-band` was written to the overlay at every change of scale and restyled
  every wire each frame: 1,293 elements a frame through the ease on the heavy
  subject. It is now written only when the camera rests. The slowed run on the
  mended build restyles none in the ease's frames on the heavy subject.

  ## N = 1.5 s, and why
  The rest is time to see what landed and bring a pointer to it, not time to
  read the card; the pointer or focus gives reading time. My count: about 0.25 s
  to take in a new picture, the name and first words at about four words a
  second (silent reading near 240 words a minute), and about 0.5 s for a hand to
  reach a large central card. It replaces the fixed 0.9 s at which the November
  21 probe found a reader pulled off the words. This is a reckoning from
  component times, not a measured optimum; the owner's eye decides.

  ## Frames on the heavy subject (`liveDocumentationConfig.ts`, 78 neighbours)
  Only the element counts are firm. The machine was at load 16 to 31 throughout,
  partly from my own lint and build.

  | Subject | Elements under the map | The look's one change, at rest after the
  ease |
  | --- | ---: | ---: |
  | estate | 1,197 | 625 |
  | `pathfind.ts` | 5,916 | 2,599 |
  | heavy | 11,965 | 6,541 |

  - The reading look alone runs through the ease in every run, and the look
  changes once.
  - **Full-speed times are the load's, not the product's**, and were taken
  before the band mend. Heavy: ease 883 to 2,350 ms in 7 to 9 frames, the change
  at rest 1.6 to 2.4 s. The quiet figures of November 21 were 27 to 32 frames
  and 450 to 467 ms.
  - **The slowed run is contaminated.** Its two cores were shared with my bundle
  rebuild, and it reported a 24 s frame on the heavy subject. Do not quote it.
  - Said plainly, as the Explorer design now says it: on a picture this size the
  look's one change holds the page after the camera stops, about half a second
  at full speed and 1.2 to 2.2 s on two slowed cores by the November 21 figures.
  Nothing moves during it. A quiet re-measurement on the mended build is owed.

  ## Pictures and motion

  val-default/` with a README, `shots.mjs`, `shots.json` and `numbers/`. Taken

  - **Estate, pointer:** after 4 s on the card the map still said `waiting`,

  to leave; 717 ms, 21 frames, longest 67 ms.
  - **Estate, focus:** first frame 61 ms after the focus left; 20 frames.
  - **Repo, focus:** first frame 66 ms after; 17 frames; the look change a 650

  - **Repo, pointer:** 4 frames in 1.7 s. That run caught the load and shows
  nothing of the motion.
  - **Wire, both samples:** the gap to the panel is 20 px, the words stood on

  My eye, on the three I opened:

  ## Other panels against the three words
  None was mended; each is in a client another builder holds. All are recorded
  in the design system.
  - **World Map peek:** it follows the pointer at 18 px and takes no pointer, so
  it is not hoverable. Escape hides it, but the next pointer movement on the
  same thing brings it back (read from the code, not run). It is persistent. It
  would take standing beside its thing as the keyboard peek does, taking the
  pointer, `onTheWay`, and staying dismissed until the pointer has been
  elsewhere.
  - **3D wire tooltip** (`showWireAt`): follows the pointer at 12 px, takes no
  pointer, has no Escape. Same three things needed.

  no Escape.
  - **Card explanations, cell names, cut counts, Tuning sentences:** native
  `title`, outside the criterion.

  ## Forks I decided
  - A pointer holds the arrival only if it moved since the arrival began, as the
  November 21 trial had it. Otherwise a pointer left where a search click put
  it would hold many arrivals by accident.
  - Focus counts only as `:focus-visible`, so a mouse click's leftover focus
  does not hold.
  - Any press, wheel or touch still ends the arrival, not only camera moves;
  opening a description ends it too. Tab, arrows, Home, End and bare modifiers
  do not end it under the new value. The older values keep "every key ends it".
  - While waiting on a reader past the rest, `data-camera` is not `moving`, so
  nothing that waits for rest hangs on a pointer.
  - Arrival Rest applies to every Arrival Waits value, so Never now rests 1.5 s,
  not 0.9.
  - The wire panel's grace is a place, not a delay.
  - A rested pointer's words take Escape first, ahead of the reader and menus.
  The design system's Escape order is amended to say so.
  - The suite-wide `AT_READING_SIZE` instead of case-by-case discovery.

  ## Forks left to you
  - Whether the suite should instead run on the true default, with cases fixed
  one by one.
  - The names of the two Arrival Waits values and of "Arrival Rest".
  - Whether Never should keep its old 0.9 s.
  - Whether a still pointer on the card should hold.

  ## Way Down trial: first milestone handback

  Branch `worktree-agent-ae93ec956c2910cd3`, reset to `cfc07502a` first, working
  tree clean. The trial is built, pictured and committed. **The World Map's,
  the search bar's and the path's existing browser specs were not run**: when I

  ### Commits
  - `bc08d3a4d` Try a way down from the World Map that carries the question,
  behind a Tuning lever (code, unit tests, Live Docs, design paragraphs,
  pictures, evidence page, the browser spec as first written).
  - `41e34e0e9` Set the Way Down lever truly in its browser cases, and picture
  this repository's board after two mends.

  ### What was built
  **The lever**: Tuning → 2D Layout → **Way Down**, *Folder Alone* (default) and
  *Carries The Question*, placed directly before Arrival. Why there: it decides
  what arrives and Arrival frames it; and that group's levers ride in a link's
  layout settings, which the chain's report needs to read the same cold. Cost:
  2D Layout is a folded group while on the World Map or in 3D, so the lever is
  one unfold away from where it acts.

  1. **A thing opens with its doors.** "open hub" pins the files on the thing's
  own end of its **call** wires and opens its folder. Estate: hub →
  `Hub/App.config` (serves the gateway, calls payments); portal →
  `Services/GatewayClient.cs`; payments → `App.config`, `Data/Payment.cs`,
  `Data/PaymentsContext.cs`. A thing no call touches (this repository's engine,
  explorer) brings the files others stand on instead.
  2. **A wire opens as its files.** A wire's pinned panel gains one link, "open
  gateway and hub", in the place and form of "open hub"; it pins every file at
  both ends. The panel's file links are unchanged. A declared wire offers none.
  3. **A chain opens as its route.** With a thing pinned on the board the first
  field shows it, "Path to…" stands, and other things are offered first by board
  name ("payments" for `PaymentService`). The answer is the Local Map's own
  path between the two folders; if only the reverse runs, the ends are exchanged
  before asking, so it is never refused. The Local Map's own bar still refuses
  and offers the reverse (tested).
  4. **The way back.** Today "open <thing>" unpins the board before leaving, so
  Back and the sidebar return to a board with nothing pinned; a wire's file
  links never unpinned. Under the lever the pin is kept, for thing, wire and
  chain, by Back and by the sidebar. The board's pin is in no address (as
  before): a board opened cold has nothing pinned.

  **Arrival framing**: one small addition to the Local Map,
  `arriveWithLines(target)`, which applies Step's existing *With Its Lines*
  frame to the opened folder (or the wire's calling card) whatever Tuning's Step
  says. No new camera maths.

  **New files**: `packages/explorer/src/client/views/worldMap/way-down.ts`
  (pure: `wireFilesOf`, `wireEndsOf`, `heldFiles`, `WAY_DOWN_CARDS`),
  `way-down.test.ts`, `tests/e2e/way-down.spec.ts`, each with a Live Doc and
  authored Purpose. Design paragraphs added to
  `.mdmd/layer-3/live-documentation-explorer.mdmd.md` (after "Which way a line
  runs"), `.mdmd/layer-3/world-map.mdmd.md` (Current visual work) and the "pin"
  row of `.mdmd/layer-2/design-system/interaction-and-accessibility.mdmd.md`.
  Nothing touched in engine, generator, the 3D client, Memory, Work, records or
  AGENTS.md. The bundle already carried the needed fact (the join's `lines` per
  wire).

  ### Addresses
  Each is the Local Map's own compressed snapshot, with `wayDown` among its
  layout settings.
  - Thing: pins = door files, opened directory = the thing's folder.
  - Wire: pins = the files at both ends.
  - Chain: opened directory = the offering thing's folder, destination = the
  other's folder.

  **Cold is not the same picture for a thing or a wire.** Same pins, folder and
  cards, but framed as any shared link is: on the newest pin at reading size,
  neighbours cut at every edge (`trial-open-hub--carries--cold.png`). The
  address carries what is pinned, not how the arrival framed it. The chain cold
  is the same path and the same report (tested in a context without the lever
  saved). The "N of M pinned" line is said on arrival only.

  The walk is already one. The page's `findPath`
  (`packages/explorer/src/client/pathfind.ts`) calls the engine's `walk(starts,
  neighbours, maxHops, goals)`, which already takes a set of starts and a set of
  goals.

  What the page has alone is turning a directory into its files: `endFiles` and
  `under` in `packages/explorer/src/client/history/moment-paths.ts`, over a
  history moment's ground. The CLI's `searchGraph` uses that same `walk`, but
  with one file each end.

  So the mend is:
  - move "the files under a directory" (the `under` predicate) to the engine
  beside `walk`;
  - let `searchGraph`, `reachFrom` and inspect's resolver take a directory as a
  set;
  - have `endFiles` call the moved predicate.

  That touches `packages/explorer`, so per your instruction I did not start it.
  I need your word to move it.

  ### Item 7
  Not reached. The ledger's `board --files` rows (a wire's files, a thing's
  files) still show "--files is not an option".

  ### The seven that need a design
  These are in the record (`.../Probes/parity/README.md`, the closing section),
  one paragraph each. The printouts there are sketches; none is built:
  - **most used files:** `inspect --most-used`, the page's `mostUsed` moved
  where both reach it;
  - **symbols nothing references:** `inspect --unreferenced`, with a first line
  saying same-file uses are not seen;
  - **a folder and how its files connect:** `inspect --folder`, its files, the
  wires among them, cycles, and outside wires counted;
  - **the pinned set:** `inspect --among a b c`;
  - **search by words:** `inspect --search`, the page's `graph-search.ts` moved,
  and whether it belongs in the engine at all;
  - **a step's authors:** `inspect --at <commit> --authors`, which needs the
  sequence to carry authors;
  - **an Explorer link:** `inspect --from X --link [--base]`.
  `?view=local&node=…` is easy; a path's `s=` needs the page's encoder, and the
  base address cannot be known by the command.
