      248  ### Reading on the map, and the Detail Panel made unnecessary

● Two of the five are settled, and nothing about either needs you:

  - The hover-band check is mended and merged (eaea23089).
  - The pin made zoomed out is not a product fault. Both pictures have the same
    79 cards, rows, pins and wires; 48 cards stand in different places, and
    because a card's rows are ordered by where their wires lead, 26 rows of the
    pinned card come in a different order. You accepted on November 16 that a
    picture may rearrange once after it arrives, and direction already sets this
    case aside for the playtests, so I ruled from that: the check now holds

› Message from general-purpose
  Fourteenth round, handed back at 22:43 UTC (begun 22:26). **Nothing of the
  product is changed.** The cause is found by recording and a mend is measured,
  but the mend changes how a view is held and put away, and I did not build it
  in the time. Commit `e3b10733e` on `worktree-agent-ad47c0821418d756a` (on top
  of `b0f2253b9`, merged first as asked) holds only the record and its measures.

  ## 1. Which read pays, and the write that makes it

  - **The read:** `changePerspective`'s second read,
  `requireElement("view-graph").getBoundingClientRect()` at
  `packages/explorer/src/client/index.ts:1377`, after the views are switched and
  the destination rendered. It took 189.7 ms with Wire Answers on, 188.0 ms
  with it off, and 172.8 ms in a third recording.
  - **The other reads:** the first read (line 1352) is not among the reads over
  4 ms. The subject's anchor (`getClientRects` of the card's title) is 11 to 14
  ms.
  - **The write:** `container.inert = !active` in `setActiveView`,
  `packages/explorer/src/client/dom.ts:48`, on `#view-map`. Making the map inert
  has the browser work out the style of all 14,810 of its elements again.
  - **How it was named:** after every write of the press I asked what the costly
  read asks, so each write paid for its own layout. `inert = true on
  div#view-map` cost 194.1 ms; every other write cost 2.4 to 23 ms, most about
  10 ms.

  It is not the hold's class, the cover, the hand's spot, the arrival's
  cancelling or the bands. On a 2D map at rest, five times each, the next read
  after a write to `#view-map` cost:

  | Write | Next read, ms (first, then four more) |
  | --- | --- |
  | nothing | 0, 0, 0.1, 0, 0 |
  | `inert = true` | 263.7, 85.0, 75.3, 78.3, 82.2 |
  | `aria-hidden` | 0 five times |
  | `active` off and `perspective-held` on | 0.4, 0.3, 0.4, 0.4, 0.5 |
  | `visibility: hidden` | 194.6, 83.7, 76.1, 84.1, 86.5 |
  | the body's `zoom-controls-active` off | 3.4, 3.1, 3.1, 3.2, 3.3 |

  The first time after a render is the dearest, and a press is always a first
  time. The arrival's default is not in it: seeded at reading size the look is
  `reading` in both, the element count is the same, and the first `inert` cost
  187.0 ms.

  ## 2. Whether it is today's

  - **The write is not today's.** The line came in `708f0cfa3` (October 20), an
  ancestor of `76cd9914c`. `dom.ts` and `perspectiveTransition.ts` do not differ
  between `76cd9914c` and the tip. In `changePerspective`, the only lines
  touching these reads and writes that differ are my two `placeReturnedSubject`
  lines, which run on the way to 2D.
  - **Yesterday's press was not measured.** I did not build `76cd9914c` in a
  second checkout; there was no time. So what makes today's press 70 to 110 ms
  longer than yesterday's 390 ms is not separated.
  - **A lead:** about 155 ms of each press comes after the handler returns, in
  the continuation that places the subject and captures the 3D scene. The box
  reads in it cost only 9.8 and 9.5 ms, so the rest is not forced layout; I did
  not attribute it.

  ## 3. The mend: measured, not built

  - **Moving the read before the write does not mend it.** The destination's box
  changes across the switch (leaving 2D takes away the zoom controls' strip),
  and the styles would only be worked out at the next frame instead, inside the
  first frame's time.
  - **What removes the cost:** a view that is not being drawn costs nothing to
  make inert. First write to a fresh 2D map, twice each:

  | First write | Next read, ms | Taken back, ms |
  | --- | --- | --- |
  | `inert` alone | 178.0, 187.8 | 154.8, 90.7 |
  | `content-visibility: hidden`, then `inert` | 2.9 + 0.1, 3.2 + 0.2 | 0.1 +
  2.6, 0 + 2.6 |
  | `display: none`, then `inert` | 13.9 + 0.1, 15.3 + 0.1 | 0 + 184.6, 0 +
  149.6 |

  - **The shape of the mend:** a held view must stay drawn, so the source would
  not be made inert at the press. While held it is kept from a reader by the
  cover (pointer) and `aria-hidden` (assistive technology), which cost 0 ms. It
  is made inert when the hold lets go, at the moment it is also taken out of
  drawing with `content-visibility: hidden`.

  **The fork for you, before anyone builds it:**
  - A held view could take keyboard focus for the length of the hold unless
  something else prevents it.
  - "An inactive view is laid out and drawn, only transparent" is a stated
  property of the hold (comment in `perspectiveTransition.ts`). Other code may
  lean on it, the 3D canvas's size while inactive among them.
  - `setActiveView` serves every view, not these two.

  I recommend building it. As measured, a mend inside `changePerspective`'s
  reads alone only moves the 180 ms into the first frame.

  ## 4. The press's time

  Before only, since there is no after. Five presses alone, timed as the case
  times it, load 0.7 before and 2.8 after:

  | | 1 | 2 | 3 | 4 | 5 |
  | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
  | the handler, ms | 332.7 | 318.7 | 296.1 | 296.7 | 311.7 |
  | with its microtasks (the case's `returned`), ms | 485.1 | 473.4 | 451.8 |
  451.1 | 467.5 |

  The page's own measures in those runs: capture 60 to 65 ms, hold 0.3 to 0.5,
  render 39 to 54. **The first frame was not measured.**

● The slow 3D press has its cause, and I ruled to mend it before landing;
  nothing needs a decision from you.

  - What it is. When you press 3D, the 2D map stays on screen under the
    transition for a moment while the 3D picture draws its first frame. At that
    instant the page marks the 2D map as unreachable by keyboard, pointer and
    screen reader, and that one mark makes the browser restyle all 14,810 of the
    map's elements: about 190 ms of the press. The line has stood since October
    20, and its cost grows with the picture.
  - The mend, measured before building. If a view is first taken out of drawing
    and then marked unreachable, the same mark costs 3 ms. So the held map will
    stay drawn and covered until the transition lets go of it, and only then be
    put away. This is done in the one place that puts any view away, so a
    sidebar switch away from a large 2D map gets the same saving.
  - What I asked to be held. A keyboard user must never be left with focus
    inside a view that is being put away. The transition must look the same both
    ways, judged by eye on this repository and the estate sample. Everything
    that relied on a hidden view still being laid out is to be listed and
    checked.
  - Not yet known. The press is also 70 to 110 ms slower alone than yesterday
    (about 390 ms then, 450 to 500 now), and that difference is not explained;
    the 190 ms above is older than today.

  The landing waits for this, because the press now fails alone some of the
  time, which is different from yesterday, when it failed only under load. The
  builder has until about 23:35 UTC; then I run this round's specs on the merged
  branch and start the four stages, so main will most likely move after
  midnight UTC, if it passes. The record is current through 993f32e5c.

❯ I just wanted to pop by with a few more ideas or things I'd hope we can save
  for later but not lose track of:
  - "Change is Golden": we currently don't have a semantic color for _change_
  that is universal across the application, and I'd propose that we choose
  something like a Gold, distinct from our Amber for warnings. I don't believe
  any nodes utilize gold currently either. My hope would be that, rather than
  changing color to white in the history view for nodes which change, perhaps
  they could glow gold instead.
  - Change as a First-Class Shareable: Right now, we have a way to show the
  state of a local map before and after a commit. I would love to be able to
  show my colleagues what the changes in my code reviews/PRs actually _look
  like_ before and after the change. All the better if they can see it _on
  their own_. Many many many permutations on how that can be done exist and
  many are dead ends or foreclose other opportunities. But it would not
  surprise me if there was immense value to be had in some shareable _thing_
  (hyperlink? HTML file? Data file? Just screenshots in an Azure DevOps comment
  like I currently do for other code review evidence of
  completion/correctness?) which made a _proposed set of changes_ very very
  easily legible to team members of any skill level. It would furthermore not
  surprise me if a shareable unit to describe changes (and I would not phrase
  it as "proposed change" or "completed change" on our onscreen text -- just
  "change") would contain multiple unique loci which the user could reasonably
  be expected to look, as one single look almost certainly would be too rich
  and dense to be understood. Just as a code review lists off changed files, so
  too could we. It is possible that we will need Live Documentation to be able
  to read manually-exported .diff files that a user has created via git or TFS
  command line utilities in order to get Live Documentation to pick it up
  (meaning we would need some data model for change of our own that is more
  agnostic to source control provider, so long as the user can use the CLI of
  their source control provider to export diffs -- and we'd be able to, via our
  CLI, do the diff ingestions efficiently when the source control in the
  workspace is git, which will be true for most users). I want you to be
  imagining what else one might be able to do with shareable units of Change,
  made to be sent to different audiences to communicate different changes. How
  can I efficiently show my colleagues exactly what the before-and-after would
  look like in a way which is immediately intuitive to the human eye? (Yes,
  changes that apply only to internals will not manifest as layout changes in
  2D or 3D or the world map; that is absolutely fine and almost certainly
  exactly the kind of detail we should be willing to lose so that we can let
  users focus on the emergent structure of interconnected files rather than
  every last little internal implementation detail. If I wanted to share the
  exact line changes and text changes, I'd just point that person to the real
  code review. We already have code review tools that are fine. This is about
  being able to show colleagues when something substantive is about to change
  or has changed, and allowing them to explore it at their pace.)

● Both ideas are saved for later, in your words, and nothing is built on either.
  They are in ideas.md (AI-Agent-Workspace/Memory/ideas.md) under "Colour and

  Change is golden

  Two things already in the design system
  (.mdmd/layer-2/design-system/visual-foundations.mdmd.md) bear on it:

  - Gold answers a weakness already written down. Today's rule is "white is the
    event", from your October 31 wish that it be obvious what is changing. Under
    it the design system records that every 3D reference wire is also white,
    and that among the marbles the white ball "reads 'unpainted' rather than
    lit".
  - The warm side of the palette is crowded. You are right that no node is gold
    in today's default palette. But the directory kind is orange and the 3D
    button's cue is amber, and those two are already noted as too close to each
    other. The Proposed palette also makes the document kind yellow. So a gold
    has to be measured against all three, and it will probably hold apart more
    easily as light (a glow, a rim) than as paint, which is what you described.

  Change as a shareable

  You asked me to imagine. The two uses I find strongest:

  - Who uses what changed. A code review lists the files that changed. It does
    not show the unchanged files that depend on them, and the map can.
  - An agent's account of what it did. Agents now produce changes faster than a
    person can read diffs. Our own guide already asks me, at every accepted
    round, for links that show you "what changed, where and why", and I make
    them by hand.

  Others, more briefly:

  - what changed while someone was away, at the World Map's scale;
  - two changes side by side, to see where they meet;
  - one emitted by a pipeline for every review;
  - several chained into the story of how something was built;
  - a change not yet made, shown from a patch.

  On the shape of it:

  - A model no provider owns. A change is two states of one folder tree, so two
    sets of Live Docs and the difference between their graphs. Source control
    only supplies the two states. In git that is any two revisions. A .diff
    gives the other state of the files it names, applied as text, with the rest
    read from disk.
  - One source, several forms. A page that carries the change and the tool, a
    link where a team hosts the Explorer, data for the command line, and
    before-and-after pictures for an Azure DevOps comment would all derive from
    the same facts. Choosing only pictures, or only the page, is one of the dead
    ends.
  - Places to look. These are the connected patches of structural change, each a
    pinned subject with its before and after. Files changed only inside are
    listed apart and said to draw no change.
  - The hard part. Before and after are only legible if nothing else moves.
    Today each state is laid out for itself, so one added file can rearrange the
    cards, and the eye sees a hundred differences of which one matters. One
    layout has to serve both states, with what is gone drawn as gone.

  When its turn comes, I would start by taking one real merged change from this
  repository and counting what the graph difference gives: how many places to
  look, and how many files changed inside only. It ranks after tonight's landing
  and the Flow and Directory trial.

  main has not moved. The builder is still on the last mend, the slow 3D press,
  and is due back about now. After that I run this round's specs on the merged
  branch and start the landing's four stages, which take about an hour and ten
  minutes.

  309.8, 301.4, 339.4, 319.9, 316.0 |

  2284.6 | 2214.7, 2181.5, 2131.4, 2145.2, 2045.6 |

  - Load: 0.24 to 2.92 around the five before, 2.09 to 2.41 around the five
  after.
  - The press of 3D is about 180 ms shorter. In the case itself, alone five
  times: 281.6, 276.9, 351.4, 260.0, 269.4 ms.
  - The sidebar pays about 230 ms less on the way out and about 155 ms more on
  the way back; the round trip is some 90 ms shorter. I did not separate whether
  the 155 ms is the map painted again or styles and layout put off while it was
  not drawn.

  ## What was built

  - **Stylesheet** (`shell.css`, `local.css`): a view not shown is
  `content-visibility: hidden`, held drawn through its 0.4 s fade by a discrete
  transition. A view shown is drawn at once. A held view is drawn.
  - **`settleViews` in `dom.ts`** is the one place a view is made inert:
  `aria-hidden` at once, `inert` only when the view's own style says it is not
  drawn. It runs at every switch, at a fade's end, at the hold's release, and
  for the modal reader. `index.ts` had made views inert a second way; that now
  goes through the same function.
  - **The hold's comment** in `perspectiveTransition.ts` and a new rule in the
  design system's `interaction-and-accessibility.mdmd.md` state the changed
  sentence. The Explorer design did not state it.

  ## The three forks

  1. **Keyboard: held without `inert`.** Before, focus was lost to the page at
  every press of 2D or 3D, during the hold and after (all ten presses measured).
     - Focus offered to a view not shown is given back to where it came from.
     - Focus standing in a view leaving goes to the sidebar's control of the
  view shown.
     - The control that began a change of perspective is disabled through it, so
  focus is lent to the sidebar's control and returned when the change is done,
  unless the reader moved it.
     - Three lines no case held were taken out. Not held: focus offered from
  outside the page, and a sidebar control that cannot take focus.
  2. **What leans on a view not shown being laid out.**
     - The destination's own box read before the switch: stands, because a
  view's own box is laid out either way.
     - The 2D key's kinds (`panels/spatial-legend.ts`, read by
  `checkVisibility`): mended there. It keeps its row while the map is not shown
  and reads again when shown. **This mend was not seen red**; it rests on the
  browser's rule.
     - `ResizeObserver`s (3D canvas, map layer, card boxes, World Map): nothing
  to answer while not drawn, the present size when drawn again. A window resized
  while 3D is put away was not tried.
     - The hidden map's paused search: `local-map-hidden` passes.
     - Reads of a box in a view not shown in `index.ts`: they ask `[inert]`
  first.
     - `inspection.spec.ts`'s inert assertions: pass.
     - Playwright's "visible" now answers hidden for elements of a view put
  away; the 35 cases run did not lean on the old answer.
  3. **One way for every view:** yes, as above. The sidebar pays the cost in the
  table.

  ## Cases, in `force-graph-focus.spec.ts`

  - **"a view leaving is not made inert while it is still drawn, and a view put
  away is not drawn and is inert"** covers the hold, the sidebar's fade, and
  focus standing in the map at a sidebar switch.
    - Red: the build before, recorded in the same properties (`perspective-held,
  inert true, drawn visible`).
    - Red: a made fault where the sidebar's control takes no focus gave
  "focusNow: a view not shown".
  - **"the keyboard's focus is never left in a view being put away nor lost to
  the page by a switch, and focus offered to a held view is given back"** uses
  Enter on 3D and on 2D, then two real Tabs from the last control before the
  held map.
    - Red: the first build, without the lending, gave "focus, as the change
  begins: the page".
    - Red: with the giving-back taken away, focus stood in "a view not shown"
  twice.
    - Three of three without a fault.

  ## The eye

  Stills, not a recording: nine screenshots through each transition, both ways,
  this repository and the estate, before and after. Twelve opened, four pairs
  kept beside the record as `put-away--before--…` and `put-away--after--…`.

  - **Held, on the way to 2D** (both pictures): the 3D picture stands drawn
  under the 2D controls, the same in both builds.
  - **Folding, on the way to 3D** (both pictures): cards and wires over the
  spheres, the same.
  - **At rest after each way** (repository, after): right.
  - **Not the same instant in one pair:** on the estate's way to 2D at about
  1.36 s, the after still is earlier in the motion by something like a tenth of
  a second. One run each cannot say whether the motion begins later. A recording
  is owed.

  Ran 2 shell commands (ctrl+o to expand)

● The line you mean is in the narrative's first pages, and I have just reread
  them: the narrative was in my context at the day's opening and compaction has
  since dropped it. On October 16, 2025 the project began as a way to give an
  agent "intuitive clues about salient context sitting just outside the bounds
  of its current windowed knowledge", and on October 19 you set its question:
  "What files will be impacted by this change?" A shareable change that shows
  who uses what changed is that same question, drawn for a person instead of
  raised as a squiggle for an agent.

  The lever you were reaching for is Churn Cost (Tuning, 2D, default 100). It
  prices each pair of cards in a column that would swap places against the
  previous picture, so it sets how much better a new arrangement must be before
  the cards move to it. One caution, saved with the idea: it prices swaps within
  a column, and a card can move far without swapping. So raising it may not be
  the whole of holding a picture still across a change; that gets measured when
  the idea's turn comes.

  Both of your follow-ups are saved beside the idea in ideas.md
  (AI-Agent-Workspace/Memory/ideas.md), and your message is Turn 17 in the
  record (e2aad1257).

  Where the landing stands

  main has not moved, and it may not move tonight.

  - The mend for the slow 3D press works but is not finished. With it the press
    takes 300 to 340 ms where it took 480 to 520. It changes how every view is
    put away, so it needs the wider runs before I merge it: 26 spec files and
    the design audit were not yet run. One cost is known: a sidebar switch back
    to a large 2D map is about 155 ms slower, against 230 ms saved on the way
    out.
  - It also mends something nobody had recorded. Keyboard focus was being lost
    at every press of 2D or 3D; it is now lent to the sidebar and returned.
  - The 3D "More" case now fails alone, and its new diagnostic says why: a
    directory shell's actions are on offer while the picture is still moving. My
    reading, being confirmed by one measurement, is that the sphere just
    pressed drifts out from under the still pointer. If so the check is wrong to
    assume a still picture, and will follow the sphere as a reader would.
  - The transition's first frame is at its 400 ms limit, before the mend and
    after. A second builder is timing it and the press on yesterday's main
    against today's, to say whether today's work slowed them and which merge
    did.

  If this round leaves those specs passing on the merged branch, I start the
  landing. If it does not, I stop and propose closing the day with main where it
  is and the landing as tomorrow's first work.

      41  ## Stewardship

      43  - **Clean workspace.** Remove your temporary outputs, and a bui
          lder's. A questionable file met during the work is investigated
           through its contents, consumers, git and chat provenance, and
          removed when no justified purpose remains; missing provenance a

  README

  - History was described as 3D only. The 2D Local Map has had the same scrubber
    since November 19. It now says the Local Map steps through commits "in 2D
    or in 3D".
  - The install-time privacy line was wrong, not just stale. It said one
    dependency runs an install script (esbuild). That is true of what the
    product itself needs, but the README's own instructions are a clone and npm
    install, which also bring two development tools that run install scripts:
    - tree-sitter-cli downloads a binary from the tree-sitter project's GitHub
      releases. It has been in the project since October 16 and is used only to
      rebuild our PowerShell grammar.
    - unrs-resolver, part of the linter, checks its native binding.

    The README now names all three and says which one the product needs. I also
    added a paragraph to SECURITY.md on the two tools and how to list them,
    since the README points there for checking.
  - The language list was missing file types. .sln and .slnx (the solution
    reader from November 21) and .vbproj and .fsproj are now listed.
  - The sample Live Doc's source line said #L56; the real doc says #L59.
  - The screenshot is stale and not yet replaced. It shows dom.ts's card from
    November 12 with three public symbols, and the caption says three. Today's
    card has five, and tonight's put-away mend adds a sixth. I will retake it
    from the landed build with a caption that counts nothing; what it should
    show is yours to judge.

  configuration keys, the guide links, and the three command-line examples,

  README either way.

    chained or scripted commands "that name git". Today three commands that

    outside the lane. The table now gives the gate as it is actually run. It

  is MIT.

  what is now happening.

  1. The language list as a table of pictures. There is no good reason it is
  words. A builder is making the table now. The Rosetta sample is one program
  written in nine languages, so the same subject in the same framing shows the
  same shape row after row. The estate sample covers the frameworks. Where no
  sample we serve shows a language (PowerShell, I believe), the row says so;
  nothing is faked. A script retakes every picture, and the stale lead picture
  is replaced with a subject that does not change under us.

  2. "Those markdown files are the product." It is true today and, by your own
  words, not planned to stay true:
  - November 19: you gave leave to try keeping the facts as what the tool
    derives, with Markdown exported ("I think it's time to try, eh?").
  - November 21: your lean was a compact binary store with text exported on
    demand, and a page that saves a copy of itself.
  - The work item "a scan leaves no trace" is open, from your "3K+ UNCOMMITTED
    FILES" worry.

  The stored form's module was built today, and nothing in the product uses it
  yet. So the README defines the product by a store that is on trial to be
  replaced, and it advertises committing the docs with your code, which is the
  thing you do not want at work. I will rewrite it so the facts are the product,
  and it says how they are kept today and what is on trial. I am leaving
  "Markdown is canonical" in AGENTS.md on purpose: direction says it stands
  until the trial is accepted, and it is the rule that stops agents adding side
  channels.

  3. Contributions. Done (713515e94): the README now says the repository is not
  open to contributions yet, and Memory holds your words so no document invites
  them.

  4. Why this is unique. The idea is not; the standard it is held to is. This is
  what I can defend from the record:
  - It is checked against the compiler, not against itself. Each language's
    reading is measured against that language's own compiler or parser, and the
    remaining differences are registered by cause.
  - It never guesses. No model is involved anywhere. What it did not read or
    cannot know, it says, and a suggestion is never drawn as a fact.
  - It is for someone who cannot read the code. The picture is the interface,
    and it is tried on strangers.
  - It reads the unfashionable code, across systems, cold. ASP.NET markup, WCF
    configuration, SQL, PowerShell and .NET Framework, with connections between
    systems drawn from configuration.
  - It is yours. Your code is read as text and never run, nothing is sent
    anywhere, and you can check that.

  Against it, honestly: the page that scans is not built, the readers for your
  own stacks were mended only this week, Ruby has no oracle, and 3D is slow at a
  thousand files. A read-only search is gathering the evidence behind each
  claim, and what weakens each; the README will claim only what a sceptic can
  open. If your own answer to "why unique" differs from mine, tell me and I will
  write yours.

  When both helpers are back I rewrite the prose and you get the README to read
  whole, with its pictures.

  The landing is unchanged: both builders are still out and main has not moved.

  - **SECURITY.md** (updated 2026-10-21):
    - Line 7: "open no sockets".
    - Line 9: "no LLM integration, no telemetry, and no update check".
    - Line 16: a grep recipe.
    - Lines 27-37: a `docker run --network none` recipe.
  - **The command line:** `.mdmd/layer-2/parity.mdmd.md:73` (2026-11-22):
  "opened no network connection under `strace`, and gave the same exit in a
  namespace with no network". The detail is in `AI-Agent-Workspace/AgentSessionH
  istory/2026/11/2026-11-22/Probes/parity/README.md:14`.
  - **What the engine needs of Node:** `tests/page-scan/node-needs.json`, a
  committed list held by `tests/page-scan/*.test.ts`. It lists no network
  module.
  - **Not found:** any test that the mapped code is never built or run. The
  claim stands only as prose (`design-philosophy.mdmd.md:37` and `40`).

  `AI-Agent-Workspace/Memory/direction.md:39` says "thirty visitor runs and two

  - The 2026-11-21 README, line 228: "Nobody went below the World Map."

  ## 6. Determinism

  - `tests/integration/live-docs/generation.test.ts:20` and `105`: "Regeneration
  should be deterministic". This is a second run over existing docs.
  - `tests/integration/live-docs/round-trip.test.ts`: parsing and re-rendering
  every doc changes nothing.
  - Page against Node: `tests/e2e/page-scan.spec.ts` compares every file whole
  against Node's output (`internal-tooling.mdmd.md:87`), and
  `tests/page-scan/differences.json` holds no differences.
  - `parity.mdmd.md:93-104` (2026-11-22) says "yes" to same bytes for `generate
  --check`, `lint` (since 2026-11-22), `inspect`, `board`, `history` and
  `visualize`.
  - **Known exception**, `parity.mdmd.md:97`: `generate` from nothing gives "no,
  every doc's `Generated At` and `index.json`'s `generatedAt` differ". The
  probe found "37 files differ, every one only in `Generated At` or
  `generatedAt`" (`Probes/parity/README.md:14`).
  - History: "1.4 times faster with every commit's result unchanged"
  (`vision.mdmd.md:125`).

  ## 7. Legacy and enterprise stacks

  - **ASP.NET markup**
  (`packages/engine/src/live-docs/adapters/aspnet.ts:25-54`, `67`):
    - Extensions: `.aspx .ascx .master .ashx .asmx .asax .svc .cshtml .razor`.
    - Directive attributes that name a file, from Microsoft's documentation,
  such as `codebehind`, `codefile`, `src` and `masterpagefile`.
    - Script `src` and element ids.
  - **.NET configuration** (`adapters/dotnetConfig.ts`, header): reads
  `Web.config`, `App.config` and `packages.config`. "appSettings keys,
  connection string names, WCF client endpoint names and WCF service names, and
  the addresses its services listen on … observed from configuration, the file
  that listens on the address each client endpoint points at."
  - **Project files** (`adapters/project.ts:68`): `.csproj .vbproj .fsproj`,
  both classic and SDK style. A classic web application is recognised by its
  GUID (line 17).
  - **Solutions:** `adapters/solution.ts` reads `.sln` and `.slnx`.
  - **SQL** (`adapters/sql.ts`, header): T-SQL binding by "its module's schema,
  then `dbo`". A linked server's name is a contract edge.
  - **PowerShell 5.1:** `adapters/powershell.builtins.ts:1-40` takes the
  commands 5.1 ships "from Microsoft's documentation" at commit `aedd8efe`.
  - **The estate sample** (`tests/integration/programs/csharp/estate/`): a .NET
  Framework 4.8 payment chain (Web Forms and Web API 2 portal → gateway → WCF
  hub → WCF service → SQL Server → Oracle), described in its README.
    - `tests/integration/live-docs/board.test.ts:22`: "draws every remote
  hand-verified edge as a wire between two things, and the declared tunnel".
    - `tests/integration/live-docs/estate.test.ts:32` and `86-88`: the estate
  scanned as seven things draws the same wires as one scan, and the hub's
  staging address is a ghost with `basis: "configuration"`.
    - Configuration-to-configuration hand-verified edges, e.g. "client endpoint
  address net.tcp://hub.onprem.example:8731/PaymentHub matches the hub's service
  endpoint" (`hand-verified-edges.json`).

  ## 8. What contradicts or weakens the claims above

  1. **"Would not have served."**
     - `vision.mdmd.md:125`: the tool run cold on that code "would not have
  served".
     - `cold-run/README.md:7` (2026-11-19): "**Not today, not without
  preparation.**" Of dbatools' 1,977 files, 1,947 stood alone; "One apostrophe
  in a SQL name stops the whole run"; "161 in DNN, none linked".
     - The readers were mended and landed on `main` on 2026-11-21
  (`readers-on-main.md:20`).
  2. **The C and C++ call gap.** `compiler-differences.json:5`: "a caller does
  not reach the file that implements what it calls … which would look like dead
  code." That is 33 + 6 missing edges.
  3. **PL/SQL is weak.** `readers-gathering/README.md:34`: 433 dependency lines
  expected, 270 right, 163 missing, 248 invented.
  4. **Oracles that cannot fail on the rule.**
     - `internal-tooling.mdmd.md:126` (PowerShell): "both sides hold the same
  rule, so a wrong rule agrees with itself". Nor can it grade "the names only
  Windows PowerShell 5.1 answers".
     - `readers-on-main.md:68`: "Windows PowerShell 5.1's half is graded by
  nothing."
     - `readers-on-main.md:84`: "Not tied: the 27 scip records". A file added to
  a scip fixture is not caught.
     - `readers-on-main.md:95`: the oracle test earlier "asserts the
  bookkeeping, not the adapter's score". This has since been mended with the
  ledgers.
     - `architectural-decisions.mdmd.md:68`: "Rosetta parity counts a C# edge as
  present when a namespace name appears in the Dependencies text, which is
  weaker".
     - The Rosetta test passes with up to 3 of 9 languages missing a canonical
  edge (≥6/9).
  5. **A guarantee broken by review.**
     - `readers-on-main.md:147`: "'No reader reads what a comment or a string
  holds' was broken by review on the morning of 2026-11-21, guarded, and broken
  again that evening by other means." 31 cases are still ledgered.
     - `stored-form-review/README.md`: "one guarantee broken five ways".
     - `engine-review/README.md` (2026-11-22): "A wrong fact shown: a path
  between ends that share files is lost."
  6. **A wrong map of the owner's own scenario.**
  `2026-11-21/Probes/blind-playtests/README.md:209-211`: `Hub/PaymentHub.cs` →
  `Hub/App.config` is not drawn, because the endpoint name is built at run time.
  "From `PaymentHub.cs` or `ServiceRouting.cs` none reaches the payment
  service." This is one of the estate's 3 ledgered misses.
  7. **Known SQL faults.**
     - `readers-on-main.md:141`: "The SQL reader binds names as if a workspace
  were one database", with 243 links on mojoPortal to another database's
  scripts.
     - `readers-on-main.md:193`: `SisterDb.dbo.Orders` is drawn as a
  same-database edge.
     - `readers-on-main.md:200`: DNN's 299 `.SqlDataProvider` files are not read
  as SQL.
     - `readers-gathering/README.md:188`: `CREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS` now
  declares no table, "with nothing said".
  8. **A grader with a fallback.** The C# compiler grader reads 34 of DNN's 82
  projects' references "from its XML's elements" because MSBuild cannot evaluate
  them (`readers-gathering/README.md:96`).
  9. **Network claim caveats.**
     - `SECURITY.md:39`: "CI cannot run that recipe".
     - The engine starts `git` as a child process: `node-needs.json` lists
  `child_process` for `gitIgnored.ts` and `gitUtils.ts`.
     - In development, the oracles build code (`dotnet build`), but that is
  maintainer tooling.
  10. **Windows not proven.** Every CI job is `ubuntu-latest`
  (`.github/workflows/*.yml`). "The same table comes from a Windows runner" is
  open (`proven-on-real-repositories.md:14`). `readers-on-main.md:88` lists "a
  run on Windows" among what was not reached.
  11. **"Inferred, the map says so."** No "inferred" mark exists. The only bases
  are `contract` and `configuration` (`coreTypes.ts:328`), plus `declared` on
  the board.
  12. **Stale or inconsistent records.**
      - `vision.mdmd.md:84` says "eight languages" and
  `internal-tooling.mdmd.md:154` "eight Rosetta implementations"; the test has
  nine.
      - `sample-programs.mdmd.md:28` (as of 2026-10-16) says C's `basics` and
  `modular` have no expectations; both now carry scip-clang records.
      - The estate README says "The `.sql` files have no compiler", but
  `expected/sql-scriptdom.json` exists.
      - `hand-verified-misses.json` says a doc comment is something "no reader
  reads", which the 2026-11-22 author's-word change revised.
  13. **Playtest findings.**
      - `AI-Agent-Workspace/Memory/direction.md:39`: which way a line runs "is
  guessed, on every rung".
      - The narrative test "scored 0.30 with the step panel and 0.28 without its
  words".

  ## What I could not find

  - The "what actually breaks when a symbol is removed" oracle named in
  `vision.mdmd.md:84`. Only that sentence and
  `architectural-decisions.mdmd.md:60` ("The details are undesigned") mention
  it. Searched across `packages`, `tests`, `scripts`, `.mdmd` and
  `AI-Agent-Workspace/Work`.
  - Any oracle for Ruby, HTML, CSS, non-manifest JSON or .NET configuration
  beyond the estate's hand-verified edges. Searched the adapter tests,
  `tests/integration/live-docs` and the sample-programs doc.
  - Any JavaScript program measured by a compiler index.
  - A test that the mapped code is never built or run.
  - Any standing run on real repositories, and any recorded run on Windows other
  than the owner's single PowerShell path case.
  - An agreement figure from a standing test for ASP.NET directives. The numbers
  come from a 2026-11-19/21 probe.

    are young.

  The evidence search corrected me, and I had answered you too confidently on
  two points:

  - "Tried on strangers" was loose. The blind playtests are models shown only
    the screen, over two hundred runs. The README now says exactly that.
  - "Checked against the compiler" is true but narrower than it sounded.

      languages' own tooling, with every difference registered by cause.

      number rather than a claim of accuracy.

      nothing, or only against a hand reading.

      agree with itself.

  do not connect a call to the file that implements it, SQL is read as if a
  folder were one database, PL/SQL is read poorly, nothing has run on Windows,
  and the real-code runs were all one-offs.

    exists; connections are marked only as read from a contract, read from
    configuration, or declared.

  by hand, and they match.

  ### The command that retakes them
  `node scripts/browser-lane.mjs --label readme -- npx tsx
  AI-Agent-Workspace/Gallery/readme/capture.mts [site] [part of a name]`
  `site` defaults to `dist/explorer` (served by the script for the run) or takes
  a preview address; I ran it with `http://127.0.0.1:8999`. One hold, 87 s, 19
  pictures, no page errors. It reuses `arriveAtReadingSize` and `localMapStill`
  from `tests/e2e/still-picture.ts` and `browserLaunchOptions`, and for the
  shipped arrival waits on the map's own `data-arrival` word (set, then gone),
  never on a timer. It is `.mts` because the repository's `.ts` runs as CommonJS
  under tsx, which has no top-level await.

  ### Decisions
  - **Which look.** Table rows: the arrival that ships (the neighbours' frame),
  because the row's point is the shape, and at reading size the neighbours are
  cut by the frame. Lead picture: reading size, where names and descriptions are
  read. So the README shows both looks once.
  - **Size.** The map's own frame, `#view-map`, 1,019 by 681 in a 1,280 by 800
  window at device scale 1: no sidebar, search bar or bottom controls. At 500 px
  wide that is 2x density; file names come out about 10 px, row names about 8.5
  px in the connections look.
  - **Subject.** `processor` in all nine Rosetta languages. In C and C++ the
  header (`processor.h`, `processor.hpp`) is the subject, since it is what other
  files include; `processor.c` alone gives a different, smaller shape.
  - **Lead picture.** `rosetta/typescript` `src/processor.ts` at reading size. I
  tried the estate's `PaymentService.cs` first: one description, wires on one
  side only, weaker. The lead is the same file the table then shows in nine
  languages.
  - **Where they live: the Gallery** (`AI-Agent-Workspace/Gallery/readme/`), not
  a dated `Screenshots/` folder. A dated folder is its day's record and must
  not be written over later, which is how the old lead went stale in the folder
  of 2026-11-12; and the README is a current document that should not depend on
  a historical folder. The Gallery's rule (one picture per subject and state,
  newer replaces older) fits. One departure, said in the Gallery README: these
  are not copies of a dated origin; their origin is the script. A new section
